#6850. 6 Safety Razors That’ll Make You Swear Off Multiblade and Electric Shaving

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

One blade gives you a smoother shave and is better for your skin.

If you shave your face with any sort of frequency (daily, every other day, weekly), chances are you use a multiblade cartridge razor—a Gillette razor is what smart money would bet on. It’s the most common option for a reason. Cartridge razors are cheap, readily available, and easy to pick up new blades for (which frequently aren’t as cheap). But if you’ve often found yourself suffering from razor bumps, ingrown hairs, or razor burn—all issues that come readily to those with coarse or thick hair—you might need a course correction. That is because multiblade razors tend to cut too close. Or, well ... it’s not that they cut too close; they cut too much. By the time three to five blades track over a hair follicle, it’s underneath the outer layer of the skin. At that point, it’s simple mathematics that some of these hairs will not grow back perfectly straight out of your skin. They get stuck underneath your epidermis and cause those big, fat ingrown pimples.

Out in the world of shaving, there are single-blade razors, safety razors, multiblade cartridge razors, hell, even electric razors. In the past decade or so, traditional single-blade razors, aka safety razors, have enjoyed a bit of a renaissance. These blades are particularly good for men with sensitive skin or that thick, coarse hair. Safety razors, since they have only one blade, are able to shave close to the skin without going overboard. That comes with some risk. It is easier to cut yourself with, since the blade is sharp and unprotected by some of the features—like moisturizing bars—that you find in cartridge razors. There’s also a pretty steep learning curve associated with them, particularly if you come from the cartridge-razor world.

For those who put in the work and get the hang of it, many say a safety razor gives a far superior shave. And they do save you money in the long run. What else are you waiting for? Oh, for me to tell you what the best ones are. Easy enough. For my money, these six are the best safety razors you can buy.

Some might say Bevel was responsible for the safety-razor renaissance of the past decade with the release of this razor (one of its very first products). The thought behind this design, and the reason many people prefer safety razors, is that they don’t cut as close to the skin as multiblade razors, which helps prevent razor bumps and ingrown hairs. That’s a great thing for men with coarse and/or curly hair, which tends to form ingrowns more easily—and it’s also great for anyone with sensitive skin.

This handy razor is on the weighty side at over four ounces, which can be a good thing for a safety razor, and the sleek design is easy to understand and use. The double-edged blades are easy to replace using the screw top. Whether you’re a safety-razor beginner or expert, this is always going to be a good pick.

If you’re new to safety razors, investing in an expensive one might not be your top priority. This budget-friendly razor is all you need in order to get the hang of safety-razor shaving. It has a traditional screw-top design that uses the same double-edged blades as some of the more expensive handles on this list. The handle has a good heft to it that, combined with an easy-to-grip handle, helps you get used to shaving at a 30-degree angle. The closed-comb design is ideal for beginners and pros alike and delivers a smooth, close shave with minimal risk of nicks and cuts. If you’re a beginner, it’s a great option to learn with. If you’re a pro, it’s a great travel option so you can leave your pricier tool at home.

If you’re starting shaving with a safety razor from scratch, there are a few things to know. The two key elements are the weight of the razor and the angle of the blade. You want a handle that is hefty and feels heavy in your hand, not so light that you’re tempted to move it too quickly over the surface of your skin. The right weight lets the blade do most of the work and allows you to shave your hair with minimal strokes and less risk of nicks. The blade angle should always be about 30 degrees, not perpendicular to your skin, so you usually want to hold the razor at a slight angle.

This well-designed razor from pubic grooming giants Manscaped takes both of those into account. The weight is perfectly hefty, and the blade is actually already designed to sit at a 30 degree angle, so you don’t need to worry as much about how you’re holding it. Both of those things make this razor a perfect jumping off point for those who are coming from the multiblade side.

Safety razors are good for a lot of things, but if you’ve ever used one to try to tackle a few days’ worth of stubble, you know it’s not always great at powering through a lot of growth—at least not with the risk of nicking or cutting yourself going up exponentially. That’s where this safety razor shines. There are six different adjustable settings that subtly widen the space between the razor and the comb, allowing for thicker and coarser hair to be shaved more easily. If you shave every day, you’ll likely want to keep it on the lowest (zero gap) setting, which is basically your classic safety-razor setting, but if you tend to go a few days between shaves, change the setting to allow you to shave your stubble more easily with fewer strokes.

Sometimes you gotta go with a classic. Merkur has been making razors in Germany for over 125 years, and you can’t argue with that pedigree. This traditional safety razor has a traditional screw-top design and a weighted head that allows you to get a silky-smooth shave pretty much immediately. There aren’t any newfangled features in this thing, but that’s kind of the point. The nonslip coating and the perfectly weighted head, as well as the closed-comb design, make this the ideal short handle traditional razor.

Up until this point, I’ve been talking about closed comb razors. The difference, as you can see, is the exposed teeth of the blade on this style, making it sharper but also more of a risky proposition. Knowing your safety razor basics is a must, but if you have the technique down, this is a way to tackle longer or coarser beards.

Don’t get me wrong, this is an expert move. For those of us who grow facial hair faster and can never get a shave as close as we want before it starts growing back, the open comb razor is one way to achieve this. The cleanest shave of your life won’t just feel great, many say it will make your hair grow back slower.

Whether or not you’re experienced with safety razors, there are a few things to take into account when choosing the right one for you.

Weight: You’ll notice that most safety razors are way heavier than the drugstore cartridge razors you may be used to, and it’s not just because they’re made of metal. That’s by design. Safety razors tend to concentrate their weight in the head, which allows them to use gravity to give you a smooth shave without applying too much pressure (which can lead to nicks and cuts). Not all safety razors are the same weight, though. Ultimately you want to find a razor that is heavy enough to give you a good, close shave but not so heavy that your hand gets tired halfway through the process.

Blades: Most safety razors will use a traditional double-edged blade. This allows you to use both sides of the blade to get a little more usage out of it. Some razors will use a single-edged blade, usually called an injector blade, which is often thicker and more durable than its double-edged counterparts. Double-edged blades are preferable, but whichever you choose, make sure refills are readily available, as you’ll need to change the blade every few uses. (Most safety razors, especially those that use double-edged blades, can be refilled with any brand of blade.)

Razor Head Type: Speaking of changing the blades, safety razors typically come with two types of head. The most common is screw-off, where you unscrew the head of the razor in order to change the blade. The downfall of these is that while they’re easy in theory, it means the head comes in multiple parts that must be unscrewed and then screwed back together, making for a somewhat complicated experience, especially for beginners. The other kind, butterfly, pops up in the middle and allows you to drop in the blade.

Open Comb vs. Closed Comb: In this case, “comb” refers to how much distance there is between the razor head and the blade itself. The more open the comb, the more aggressive the shave. Closed comb razors are especially ideal for beginners because they help cut down on the risk of nicks and cuts (as there is no space for skin to get caught). Open-comb razors are ideal for people with thicker beards or longer stubble, as they allow more hair to reach the blade at once, but they carry a higher risk for nicks and cuts.

Adjustable Settings: Some razors will allow you to adjust the settings to take it between a closed and an open comb. Usually this involves different inserts that push space between the blade and the razor head. While not always necessary for everyone, this is ideal if you like to allow time between shaves and sometimes shave with a few days’ worth of stubble or more.

#6848. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

#6843. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#6840. How One Mistake Unleashed a Horde of Night of the Living Dead Bootlegs

A simple copyright error allowed George A. Romero’s zombie classic to enter the public domain. For years, the filmmakers fought to get the rights back.

would you rather accidentally set your arm on fire with spilled gasoline or spend decades grappling with a paperwork mistake? Gary Streiner, who personally experienced both while making 1968’s Night of the Living Dead, says there’s no contest: “Oh, believe me, I would take the burn again.”

Streiner was the sound man on George A. Romero’s classic indie zombie flick, but since everyone on the makeshift Pittsburgh project did multiple jobs, he also helped create visual effects, like the fireball on a porch chair that would later engulf his limb. His brother, Russ Streiner, was another multitasker, playing the taunting brother Johnny in the movie’s opening, and serving as jack-of-all-trades co-producer. Both were loyal Romero confidantes who helped him found Image Ten, the production company that created the apocalyptic undead tale.

So both were among those bitten in the ass when Night of the Living Dead accidentally ended up in the public domain due to a copyright mistake that was anything but simple to fix. Even today, it hasn’t entirely been resolved, which is why there is a right way and a wrong way to watch the movie (at least, if you care about seeing it in the best possible format, while also not picking the pocket of the people who made it.)

Today, Romero is gone, and his debut feature is considered an all-time American classic. Stephen King himself calls it the scariest movie he’s ever seen. But the independent film garnered such success partly because it was so widely and freely shared. The friends and colleagues who helped Romero make Night of the Living Dead have finally figured out a way to pull the movie back into their legal possession. But the freebie versions are still out there, so they are hoping fans will be honest and honorable about the version of the film they choose to play this Halloween.

Don’t worry, they aren’t “coming to get you, Barbara,” as Russ intones in the iconic opening of the movie. They are just, once again, asking nicely.

Basically, look for the Criterion Collection logo. The beloved cinema preservation group helped the Streiners restore the original black-and-white negative for the Museum of Modern Art, and by refining the moody imagery as Romero originally intended, they technically created a “new” work that Image Ten could officially claim as their own. You can find it on the Criterion streaming service, but also HBO Max is among those that license their version of the movie.

“All of it’s licensed now. Now we get paid for it. That’s the point, every time it’s screened on any of those platforms,” Gary says.

“There are other streaming channels that aren’t,” adds Russ. If you don’t spot that big, silver C when Night of the Living Dead starts playing, you’re probably watching a bootleg.

The average joe might not care much whether these aging producers or Romero’s estate get their share, but keep in mind that these were average joes too, not Hollywood big shots. They were working stiffs who had a dream, made something immortal (in many senses of the word), and then got stiffed for real.

“To be honest with you,” Russ begins. “George—and I want to measure my words pretty carefully …”

“He was not a great businessman,” Gary cuts in, not measuring his words at all.

“None of us were great businessmen,” Russ adds with a sigh.

The Streiner brothers were toiling away on local Western Pennsylvania commercials when they helped Romero bring his feature film dream together, and their collective work inspired generations of other nobodies to max out their credit cards, load up on cameras and lights, and make a crazy movie with their friends. “It’s fair to say that without George A. Romero, I would not have the career I have now,” Shaun of the Dead and Hot Fuzz filmmaker Edgar Wright wrote when Romero died in July 2017. “A lot of people owe George a huge debt of gratitude for the inspiration. I am just one of many.”

It’s easy enough to find the right version of Night of the Living Dead, and it happens to be the one that looks the best too. Many of the bootlegs are copies of copies of copies. When the Streiners got MoMA and Criterion to revive a new version of the movie, they went back to the original negatives, part of which they possessed, and some of which belonged to Romero’s cowriter. “We gathered up all of the elements from Russ’s basement and John Russo’s garage, and we trekked out to their facility, and we were all quite amazed at the good condition that everything was still in, not having been kept in absolute ideal conditions,” Gary says.

Now it looks as good as it did when the very first prints were sent out into the world. That also happened to be the moment everything went wrong.

You know how old movies have a noticeable copyright tag in their opening credits, usually right under the title? Today, that’s an affectation, used by filmmakers like Quentin Tarantino and Rian Johnson to conjure a feeling of nostalgia. But once upon a time in real Hollywood, that was actually a legal requirement to establish ownership of a film.

Romero and his team understood this, and had it on their initial cut of the movie. The mix-up happened when they were negotiating to get the zombie picture into theaters and struck an agreement with the indie distribution company the Walter Reade Organization. “That was the beginning of our nightmare,” Russ says. “The movie at that time was called Night of the Flesh-Eaters. They didn’t like the title. They said, ‘We’ve got to come up with something new.’ ”

The executives had a point: Night of the Living Dead has a certain ring to it, so Romero and the Streiners agreed. They left the print in Walter Reade’s hands. “They were in charge of changing the title, but when the title got changed, the copyright was left off,” Russ says. “It became their responsibility to deal with their lab, and the copyright notice for Night of the Living Dead went by the wayside.”

No one realized the mistake until the prints were shipped out, and by that point it was too late. “Not that ownership was ever denied,” Gary says. “There’s never been any, ‘Well, no, you didn’t make Night of the Living Dead.’ We made it.”

Under the United States’ 1909 copyright law, this technical mistake just meant no one was legally obligated to account for tickets or pay them a portion of the sale. “That was the start of it, and we’ve been spending tons of money legally to get this thing on its right feet,” Gary adds.

If that seems both unbelievable and unfair, then welcome to the copyright law revision of the mid-1970s that eliminated this requirement. “It’s totally different now,” says Russ. “As a matter of fact, Night of the Living Dead was one of the reasons that the language for the copyright office changed, because it was preposterous.”

But it was too late for the zombie film and the team behind Image Ten, until the new restoration came about from MoMA and the Criterion Collection. The Streiners learned this lesson the hard way, but they were used to that.

Who could have guessed that a simple line of text could cause so much trouble? Well … who could have guessed that when you pour gasoline on a chair for a zombie attack scene that it could somehow also go wrong.

“George didn’t like the way it was looking, and it took about a half hour for him to reset and get the shot that he wanted,” Russ recalls of the infamous incident. “So the chair was put out, and when it came time to do another take in the new camera position, I had been all over that thing diligently making sure that there were no embers or whatever.”

He didn’t think about what was below the surface. “There was no fire on the chair itself, but the interior workings of the chair, the springs and the metal framework, were still hot,” Russ adds. “And when Gary started dousing it with gasoline—BOOM!—it was like an explosion that set him on fire.”

“I’m literally pouring gasoline out of a plastic jug, and the flame shot right up,” Gary says, mimicking the motion.

Spilled gas on his sleeve meant his whole arm was engulfed. “Fortunately, Bill Hinzman, who was a cemetery ghoul, was actually a volunteer fireman, and he had enough common sense to just tackle me and roll me to the ground,” Gary says.

It was over in seconds, with only relatively minor burns that eventually healed. The copyright conflagration took a lot longer to resolve, but now, finally, they don’t feel so scorched by it anymore.

#6838. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6832. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6829. Patrick Stewart Remembered as a Colossus of Stage and Screen

“He wore his heart on his sleeve, which made him a man to rely on, to admire and in my case, love,” Ian McKellen shared following the actor’s death.

Actor, writer, and activist Sir Patrick Stewart died on Wednesday. He was 86. In a statement issued via his social media on October 29, his family wrote that the Star Trek star and renowned Shakespearean thespian left us following “a brief and poignant journey” with Alzheimer’s disease and Lewy body dementia. “He will be remembered for the dignity, compassion, and humanity he brought to every role—in his art and life,” the statement read.

Since the news surfaced, an outpouring of grief rippled from his closest colleagues and collaborators. Notable among them, Sir Ian McKellen, a true friend and screen partner of Stewart’s for over 50 years across stage and film, wrote an emotional tribute to Stewart over social media.

“Before everything else, Patrick was defined by being born and bred in Yorkshire, the proud county in the north of England which he loved to the end,” McKellen wrote. “He wore his heart on his sleeve, which made him a man to rely on, to admire and in my case, love.”

In 1987, Stewart was already a renowned stage actor in the UK with a handful of credits in movies like Excalibur and David Lynch’s Dune when he landed his star-making role: Captain Jean-Luc Picard in Star Trek: The Next Generation. The series ran until 1994 and made Stewart a household name. Then, he starred in the summer blockbuster X-Men (2000), playing mentor Charles Xavier with McKellen opposite him as Xavier’s once-close friend turned philosophical antagonist, Magneto.

Fellow actors who worked with Stewart across Star Trek, Marvel, and beyond also penned tributes and personal anecdotes in memory of Stewart. The Next Generation co-star LeVar Burton wrote in a public statement: “He was family. There is no other context in which to hold him that makes sense. I am lucky to have known and loved so many of the facets of a soul so rich in talent and generous of heart. Of all the dames and knights I’ve known, he was deep down the silliest.”

Hugh Jackman, who starred in several X-Men installments with Stewart—including their acclaimed 2017 hit Logan—wrote on Instagram: “We lost one of the greats of stage and screen today. And, in my book, a tremendous friend and man. I learned so much from him. I will miss you, Patrick Stewart, all of my days.”

Television creator Seth MacFarlane, a noted Trekkie who cast Stewart in a handful of bit parts across Family Guy, American Dad, and his 2012 R-rated comedy Ted, said Stewart was “that rare actor who could take any bit of dialogue and elevate it, making a good writer sound great, and a great writer sound masterful.”

Wrote MacFarlane: “I remember exchanging emails with him a number of years ago when he was beginning day 1 of a new film. He commented to me that he always walked onto a new set feeling like the biggest novice there. My stunned reaction was the same as anyone’s would be. I said, ‘Patrick, if you feel that way, the rest of us are screwed.’ We have lost a legendary actor today, and for many of us, a good friend.”

Dafne Keen, who worked with Stewart extensively in Logan as a child actress, remembered the late performer on her Instagram: “He was such a kind, genuine, funny, unique, caring and charming man. I remember his endless jokes, nurturing nature and colourful reading glasses. Every single day spent working by his side was an honour and a privilege I can’t quite believe I had. He was a walking master of his craft, and of life. One of the greatest of our time, on stage and on screen.”

She continued, “I will forever hold those months we spent together close to my heart, and will always think of him as a pivotal part of how I view the world around me.”

Shortly after the news of Stewart’s passing broke, players on Star Trek Online gathered in the game’s social areas to hold a virtual vigil in Stewart’s honor.

Below, you can read more social media remembrances from Stewart’s many colleagues and creative partners, as well as theaters that were lucky enough to have Stewart grace their stages. As Jean-Luc Picard once said, “Make now always the most precious time. Now will never come again.”

#6827. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6820. Is Walton Goggins Leaving Fallout?

"Just took it off for the last time" the actor wrote in a cryptic social media post.

Walton Goggins may have just said goodbye to Fallout. Though Prime Video hasn’t made any announcements yet about wrapping production on the upcoming third season, Goggins posted a video to social media on Thursday, October 29, that alludes to the end of his time on the video game adaptation. At least, that’s what we think it means...

“Just took it off for the last time.. Season 3… Gatdamn this felt good,” Goggins captioned a video of himself driving through the California desert, lip-syncing to “Ramble On” by Led Zeppelin. “I wish this road or this song would never end,” he continued. “Gonna miss you California… til the next time. #Fallout.”

While it’s clear that Goggins is finished with season 3, his phrasing implies that he’s wrapped on Fallout entirely. “Just took it off for the last time” sounds like a permanent goodbye for The Ghoul—his gun-slinging character from the Fallout series who is transformed into a post-apocalyptic mutant. His caption could also imply that Fallout season 3 marks the end of the series, though that would be a bit of a stretch considering the show’s sustained popularity and acclaim. Prime Video has yet to comment.

Whatever Goggins means by “the last time,” at least we know now that Fallout season 3 is closer than ever. It’s been a long year since season 2 premiered on Prime Video, with the finale releasing in January earlier this year.

Fans are dying to see what will become of Lucy (Ella Purnell), Maximus (Aaron Moten), and everyone’s favorite noseless bounty hunter. In the season 2 finale, Lucy’s father, Hank (Kyle MacLachlan), triggered a switch that made him lose all memory of himself and his time at Vault-Tec. That couldn’t have come at a worse time, as Caesar’s imposing Legion approaches the ruins of New Vegas. As for Goggins’ Cooper Howard, he’s on his way to Colorado, chasing the slim chance his family may still be alive.

In January, Goggins told Esquire that Cooper is in the pursuit of hope, odds be damned. “What happens [to the Ghoul] is what happens to all of us, which is we hope," he said. "Even before we had language, [we had hope] of finding warmth or shelter or what's on the other side of that rock. That's just who we are as people.”

Let’s hope that Goggins’ time on Fallout isn’t the end. But if it is, he better get the greatest send-off we’ve ever seen on television.

#6810. The 50+ Best Deals from Amazon’s Pre-Black Friday Sale

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Markdowns on Apple, designer watches, and the Samsung Frame are worth buying early.

In the good old days, Black Friday was one simple (albeit chaotic) day a year; now it’s a whole month of debating whether or not we should take the plunge on a deal or wait for it to get better. Who has the time? We do, because it’s our day job. We spent hours browsing Amazon’s pre-Black Friday deals so you don’t have to.

Here’s the deal: For now, select Apple tech, the best art TVs (including the Samsung Frame), several respectable designer watches, and our all-time favorite T-shirt are already on decent sale, so you don’t have to wait on those. But sit tight on things like Kindles, Ray-Ban Meta glasses, and big-ticket furniture—for now.

Expect Amazon to ramp up Apple deals throughout the month (we’re holding out hope for the already-cheap MacBook Neo to go on sale for the first time and are almost positive the AirPods 5 will drop below $100), but for now, these are the only Apple deals remotely deserving of your attention. FYI: Apple AirTags are currently the cheapest they’ve ever been.

Art TVs are reigning supreme when it comes to Amazon tech deals right now—perfect for watching Thanksgiving football or displaying a fireplace screensaver over the holidays. Several of our favorite art TVs are already marked down, including Hisense, Samsung Frame, and TCL. (Though the Frame deal stands to steepen.) Kindles haven't been discounted yet, but we expect them to be on Black Friday.

Esquire editor staples like Lacoste polos and Levi’s jeans are already heavily discounted on Amazon. Shop early to secure a Thanksgiving outfit that’ll impress those random family members you rarely see. You’ll have better luck shopping for running shoes and workout gear if you wait.

Amazon sales are our secret source for great watches. Right now, you can save up to 40 percent on timepieces from Citizen, Bulova, Casio, Tissot, Timex and other respectable makers. Additional models will be discounted around Cyber Monday, so don’t give up hope if the one you’re eyeing is still full price.

LEGO sets make for crowd-pleasing gifts for that niece or nephew of yours (or, you know, older brother), but we’re just as likely to keep these discounted sets for ourselves. (Hey, anything to take a screen break.) We’ve seen LEGO deals sell out fast in years past, so shop early while stock is plentiful.

Amazon’s pre-Black Friday sale can also help you prepare your home for holiday hosting duties. You can bet on finding cleaning gadgets, cookware sets, leaf blowers, mattresses, coffee makers—you name it—for less than full price in November. Deals on large furniture items like sofas and dressers are so-so as of now, but that should change in the coming weeks.

Airline travel is expensive enough, so don’t waste money on full-priced luggage. Samsonite, Troubadour, and other popular brands are already offering discounts on checked suitcases, carry-ons, duffels, and backpacks—but Away and Monos are still full price, so stand by if you have your heart set on those.

Skip a trip to the store (and save a few bucks in the process) by picking up personal care products during your next Amazon haul. Now is a great time to invest in a new electric razor or restock your cologne, but we’d wait to buy beauty gifts for the women in your life until closer to Black Friday.

FYI, Madden NFL 27 and NBA 2K27 games for the latest Xbox and PlayStation models are currently discounted on Amazon (though sadly, the gaming systems themselves are not—yet). Supportive swivel chairs, curved computer monitors, over-ear headphones, and other gaming essentials are also on sale. Upgrade your setup now so you can enjoy it over the holiday break.

In this economy, who can afford to spend full price on holiday gifts? Shop wisely before Amazon’s Black Friday-Cyber Monday sale, and you can check loved ones off your list early for up to 40 percent less. If the gifts you’ve already been eyeing yourself are still full price, hold off; there’s a strong chance they’ll be marked down in the coming weeks.

#6805. Next-Gen Mineral Surfaces for Your Kitchen, Bath, and More

Éclos by Cosentino merges natural beauty and breakthrough technology.

As a homeowner, the two things you tend to expect most from your ideal kitchen countertop are all too often in conflict: You want it to be truly beautiful, to evoke elemental, one-of-a-kind natural stone. But you also want all the resilience, innovation, and eco-friendliness that 21st-century technology can provide.

Renowned sustainable-surfaces brand Cosentino has made its reputation bridging those desires, and its Éclos line is both good-looking and good for the planet, simultaneously timeless in appeal and tech-forward in performance. That’s exactly what made it a surface of choice for many of the rooms in the House Beautiful Whole Home in Charleston, South Carolina, an effort of our sister publication. Here’s what makes Éclos the next generation of surfaces:

Unlike traditional stone that is cut from a quarry and installed in discrete slabs, Éclos is manufactured from a combination of minerals fused together with a proprietary technology the company calls Inlayr—the name of which gives rise to this new category of high-tech materials: inlayered mineral surfaces. Advanced robotics allow the surfaces to be imbued with sophisticated, three-dimensional veins and patterns that echo those of precious materials pulled from the earth. The result is the unique appearance of prized stone like marble or granite, with a strength that natural stone could never achieve on its own.

So what does that mean once you actually install Éclos in your kitchen or bathroom? For one thing, far less maintenance than might be required of natural materials. Éclos is nonporous, so it never needs to be sealed, much less resealed every six months to a year as, say, you might need to do with a marble counter. And it’s as solid as a rock—wait, more solid than rock: In the kitchen, it can withstand temperatures up to 428°F without showing signs of damage such as stains or cracks. In the bathroom, a knocked-over bottle of nail polish remover is no big deal. Dings, scratches, stains... things of the past.

One of Cosentino’s core values as a company is sustainability—the brand devotes a large portion of its research and development resources to discovering new and better ways to employ recycled materials and reduce its overall carbon footprint.

In the case of Éclos, Cosentino says the development of the surface involved nearly 30,000 hours of research and testing. The result is its most advanced product yet; Éclos uses zero crystalline silica and a minimum of 50 percent recycled materials (in some products in the new line, that goes up to almost 90 percent). That’s welcome news for designers and homeowners who are committed to incorporating sustainable, socially and environmentally responsible materials and practices into their renovations and new builds.

Of course, when it comes to creating a space you love and will be happy living with for years, state-of-the-artness only goes so far—style and sophistication are the other half of the equation. It’s a principle Cosentino, which also created designer-beloved modern surfaces like Silestone and Dekton, has doubled down on. The Éclos line is characterized by a carefully curated selection of quietly luxurious colors and textures, each crafted to mirror the richness and tactile appeal of quarry-cut stone. The surfaces have a visual depth and luster that almost make you forget how hardworking they are.

For kitchens and bathrooms—anywhere you can envision the sophistication of stone—Éclos can be key to crafting a space that is high-end but never high-maintenance. It's the future of surfaces, delivered right now.

#6800. Meet the ‘Dexter: Resurrection’ Season 2 Cast & Characters

Just in time for Halloween, one of television’s bloodiest (in sooo many ways) leading men is back. Ready to dive into Dexter: Resurrection season 2? Here’s everything you need to know about the Dexter sequel series cast and characters.

Fans of Dexter who hated the antihero’s fate in the original series finale probably feel so vindicated right now. Has there ever been a bigger “never mind” in pop culture? I don’t know! You honestly might as well call this show Dexter: Retcon. It is a sequel to Dexter: New Blood, which was also of course a sequel to Dexter. The first season took the “good” serial killer to New York City in search of his grown-up son. But fatherly duties can’t keep him from pulling out the plastic sheets. A new city actually leds to so, so many opportunities for dark vengeance. In that first season, Dexter met a “club” of serial killers lead by a character played by Peter Dinklage. What next? Season 2 promises an equally impressive murderers’ row.

Spoiler alert, but you’ll still see David Zayas play Angel Batista, even though he died at the end of Season 1, in some capacity this season. You’ll also continue to see James Remar play Harry Morgan, Dexter’s deceased father and spiritual guide. As for the other main characters, new and returning, here’s a who’s who.

Dexter is, as I’m sure you know, a serial killer whose adopted father Harry “trained” to have a strict moral code and only murder other killers and dangerous criminals.

Michael C. Hall has now played his best-known character on Dexter, Dexter: New Blood, Dexter: Original Sin, and Dexter: Resurrection. Before that, he was already pretty well known for playing David Fisher on HBO’s Six Feet Under. He played John F. Kennedy in one episode of The Crown. He is a Golden Globe and SAG Award winner. Hall is also a winning stage actor whose Broadway credits alone include Chicago, Cabaret, and Hedwig and the Angry Inch. Theatre-lovers also know that he starred in the David Bowie musical Lazarus off-Broadway and in London. And some of his film credits include Game Night, Kill Your Darlings, and Christine.

Harrison is Dexter and Rita’s now grown-up son. This season he’s joining the NYPD.

Jack Alcott first started playing Harrison in Dexter: New Blood back in 2021. He was also a lead on the miniseries The Good Lord Bird. He guest starred in Poker Face and The Blacklist. To this date, he only has one film credit: the 2025 supernatural western Killing Faith.

Charley is a security guard who worked for a rich but late serial killer.

Over 20 years later, Uma Thurman is still probably best known for the Kill Bill duology and Pulp Fiction. Let’s never, ever forget that she played Poison Ivy in Batman & Robin, either. She is an Academy Award-nominated and Golden Globe-winning actress. While she’s not really known for television, some of her previous small screen credits include Suspicion, Hysterical Blindness, Chambers, NBC’s The Slap, and NBC’s Smash. It’s very possible that the last thing you saw her in was Red, White & Royal Blue as the President of the United States... NBD!

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine plays Blessing Kamara

Blessing is a rideshare driver, Dexter’s landlord, and first friend in NYC.

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine played Ronnie Davis on The Chi for 30 episodes and Jacques Jhoni on Tremé for 26 episodes. His other television credits include Heroes, The Riches, The Knick, and The Lincoln Lawyer. On the big screen, you may have seen him in Blood Diamond, Queen of Katwe, or Farewell Amor.

Claudette is an NYPD detective who worked with Harrison in season 1 and could pose a threat to Dexter for obvious reasons.

Kadia Saraf played a U.S. Attorney on seven episodes of Law & Order: Special Victims Unit and reprised her role on Law & Order: Organized Crime. She popped up on many other procedural shows before and has continued to since, including FBI, Blue Bloods, Rescue Me, and The Good Fight.

Fiona is an NYPD training officer who befriends Harrison. Her father, played by Bokeem Woodbine, is the captain.

Prior to Dexter: Resurrection, Nona Parker Johnson also played a police detective on Law & Order: Organized Crime. Her other television credits include recurring roles on Fear the Walking Dead, Mayor of Kingstown, and Sugar.

Krysten Ritter plays Mia LaPierre aka Lady Vengeance

Mia is a member of a former social club for serial killers who met and befriended Dexter in the first season. Dexter has not decided to “do what he does” with her yet.

Krysten Ritter is doing double duty on the fictional streets of New York City these days. She also plays the street-level superhero Jessica Jones on Daredevil: Born Again, reprising her role from Netflix’s various Marvel series in the Defenders universe. She’s also known for Don’t Trust the B in Apt 23, Breaking Bad, Veronica Mars, Gilmore Girls, Orphan Black: Echoes, 27 Dresses, Mona Lisa Smile, and Vamps.

Brian Cox plays Don Framt a.k.a. the New York Ripper

Don is a former serial killer taunting his “alleged” victims.

Hopefully you know Brian Cox from Succession and not just the McDonald’s commercials... but they are very good commercials! He is an Olivier, Emmy, BAFTA, and Golden Globe-winning actor. His film credits include The Fantastic Mr. Fox, Her, Zodiac, Rise of the Planet of the Apes, X2, Troy, Adaptation, and Braveheart. Prior to Dexter and Succession, his television credits included War & Peace, Penny Dreadful, The Straits, Kings, Deadwood, and an Emmy-nominated guest turn on Frasier.

Dan Stevens plays Lucas Stark a.k.a. the Five Borough Killer

rLucas is another new serial killer this season. He is actively killing and wants attention. Or he’s willing to risk attention to prove that the NYPD can’t catch him even with ample warning.

Dan Stevens was best known for playing Matthew Crawley on Downton Abbey. His career has kind of taken only left turns since. (Metaphorically. I know geographically that’s a circle.) He played the Beast in Disney’s live-action Beauty and the Beast. He starred as the Marvel Comics antihero David Haller on the FX series Legion. He had a leading role in the recent season of The Terror, and his other film credits include The Guest, Abigail, Eurovision, Cuckoo, and the recent Godzilla x Kong movies.

Gabriel Luna plays Ray Ballard a.k.a. the Sleepy-Eyed Stranger

Finally, Ray is a third new serial killer this season. Will he be bested by Dexter, or be the one to best him? We’ll have to wait and see.

Luna is best known these days for playing Tommy on HBO’s The Last of Us. He was also a version of the Marvel comics character Ghost Rider on Agents of S.H.I.E.L.D. and played a Terminator called Rev-9 in Terminator: Dark Fate. You might also know him from shows like Matador, The Terminal List, True Detective, and Wicked City.

#6799. Amazon’s Black Friday AirPods Deals Dropped Early

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Shop surprise discounts on the AirPods Pro 3 and AirPods Max 2 long before Thanksgiving.

While the Black Fridays of yore involved a sea of people rushing into a big-box store to score a new TV or gaming console at a record-low price, the biggest shopping holiday of the year looks a bit different these days. Now, the madness is largely confined to the internet, and the sales happen all month long, not in the wee hours when you’re fighting off a turkey-induced nap.

Case in point: Amazon’s current slate of Apple deals let you save an impressive amount on AirPods long before you’ve even finalized your Thanksgiving plans. Every time Apple gadgets, namely AirPods, go on deep discount, they tend to sell out quickly. It’s just like those Black Friday mad dashes, just from the comfort of your own couch.

Right now, both the AirPods Pro 3 and the AirPods Max 2 are on sale, although we suspect that these deals might deepen soon. If you’re looking for the cheapest possible pair, the brand-new AirPods 5 are just $129—a great deal at full price—but we predict they’ll go on sale for the first time in the coming weeks.

AirPods Pro 3 are Apple’s most advanced earbuds yet. We called them “pretty damn cool” back when they first dropped in September 2025, and more than a year later, we stand by it.

Not only do they come armed with the noise cancellation Apple is famous for, but they also boast both adaptive and transparency audio features that adjust to the environment around you. Krista Jones, senior commerce director, is partial to the adaptive mode, which she says is “slightly less noise cancelling and always tunes into my surroundings to turn things up or down accordingly.”

Jones said that the Live Translation feature has become particularly useful in her bilingual home. Heart Rate Sensing is also of note—it keeps you plugged into your heart rate during workouts without having to wear a fitness tracker.

While a fourth generation of AirPods Pro were rumored to drop in fall 2026, there’s been no sign of them in Apple’s most recent slate of releases, so if you’re looking for the most sophisticated AirPods on the market to date, these are it. We always recommend buying them when they drop under $200, but they’ve retailed for as low as $169 during other sales, so there’s the chance this price could drop more come Black Friday.

Should you prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $100 off in select colorways as part of Amazon’s pre-Black Friday deals. Back when the first generation of these dropped in 2022, editor-at-large Dave Holmes called them “Apple’s best product in years.” Four years later, Apple’s second stab has only improved the superb noise cancellation and sound quality we already loved, and added in a slate of beloved features from the AirPods Pro (think Live Translation, Conversation Awareness, and Personalized Volume), without sacrificing the sleek build.

Since the second generation was released in March 2026, the lowest we’ve seen them go is $398. Ahead of Black Friday, a $50 price cut is nothing to scoff at, especially in an age where our favorite tech has fallen prey to price increases, but you don’t need to rush either. We’ll be keeping a close watch on these.

And while the just-released AirPods 5 aren’t on sale yet, they’re still far and away the cheapest model you can get if budget is top of mind—even if you splurge on the version with a wireless charging case.

Unlike Apple’s older generations of entry-level earbuds, these have active noise cancellation, which was previously only available in premium models. They also have Live Translation and improved dust-, sweat-, and water-resistance, making them earbuds with a premium feel—sans the price tag. We’re anticipating that these will go on sale for the first time on Amazon just in time for Black Friday.

Our rule of thumb when it comes to shopping Apple tech for Black Friday? If there’s something you’ve been eyeing at a price you can stomach, it’s worth adding to your cart. When AirPods go on deep discount, they tend to sell out quickly, and then you’ll be stuck with your tired old earbuds until next year.
