#6798. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.

Every Sunday evening and Monday morning this November and December, we’re sure to be talking about what the cast of New York’s finest stage actors has gotten up to on The Gilded Age. The season will have eight episodes total, similar to all previous seasons except for the first one, which had nine. This means that The Gilded Age season 4 finale will air on December 20, right in the middle of the holiday season.

#6792. The Surprise It: Welcome to Derry Episode Is Crawling with Stephen King Lore

HBO dropped a special Halloween episode over the weekend, which tells the origin story behind the origin story.

You might want to keep some of your Halloween decorations up a bit longer, because there’s a treat still hiding at the bottom of your candy bag. While everyone was out trick-or-treating on Saturday, October 31, HBO released a surprise new episode of It: Welcome to Derry. And yes, it’s scary, spooky, and very much worth watching. Especially for Stephen King lorehounds.

Titled It: Welcome to Derry — A Story of Derry, the special episode is a standalone prequel that reveals how the Sqoteawapskot locked Pennywise away in the forests of Derry. It is framed as the “real” story, unsanitized by the “gentler tale” that the Sqoteawapskot tribe cooked up for the sake of the children. “This is the story we do not teach,” reads the introduction text. “This is the part we left out.”

Kiawentiio (Avatar: The Last Airbender) reprises her role as Neekani, who previously appeared through flashbacks in season 1. But in A Story of Derry, Neekani is now the main protagonist. She defies her elders to hunt in the forbidden Western Wood and help replenish their dwindling food supply.

For most of the special, Neekani and her friends brave their encounter with “It”—also the Gallo—who is growing more powerful now that it’s feasting on English settlers. There’s also a creepy dude named Mitig (played by Zahn McClarnon) who is the first to learn that the shards of It’s giant crystal ship can ward the monster away. There’s also a predictable but nasty plot twist involving Mitig’s true motives, which I won’t give away.

But the real draw is the live-action debut of a pretty important character from Stephen King’s mythos: Maturin. He appears to Neekani after she smokes some of Mitig’s herbs, and he informs her how her tribe can contain It for at least a few generations.

A character from both It and King’s The Dark Tower series, Maturin is a benevolent turtle-like deity powered by “The White.” His goodness, purity, and light stand in opposition to It’s darkness. He has a small but pivotal role in the novel, telepathically communicating with Bill and protecting the children during the Ritual of Chüd. He dies at some point after choking on a universe. Which kind of feels like a joke on the part of Stephen King, given that turtles don’t chew.

A Story of Derry is the quickest half hour you might spend this week. It’s a solid piece of American Indian horror, which remains a criminally rare occurance throughout mainstream pop culture. (The closest I can think of is 2022’s Prey, which is barely a horror flick, though A Story of Derry feels cut from the same cloth.) There are heavy allusions to the traumas of colonization, as well as the loss of land and way of life.

While Bill Skarsgärd doesn’t appear as Pennywise, sadly, “It” puts on a scary face as a creepy, pox-riddled Christian missionary who taunts Neekani’s mother. Still, like a bar of Twix at the bottom of a trick-or-treat pail, A Story of Derry is a most welcome surprise. Now we’re left wondering when season 2 will premiere. What’s the over/under on a Christmas surprise?

#6788. Beyoncé Looks Incredible in a Green Tinsel Skirt and Tropical Blouse at Jay-Z’s Show in Los Angeles

Beyoncé doesn’t dress for events like other people. So, when she made a surprise appearance on night one of Jay-Z’s back-to-back shows at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California, last weekend, she did so in a fresh-off-the-runway look, naturally.

#6785. Katie Holmes and Jason Bard Yarmosky Look Wildly in Love as They Hit the Red Carpet at the American Ballet Theatre Fall Gala

The actress was elegant in a green Kallmeyer dress

The David H. Koch Theater in Lincoln Center was filled with love last night as Katie Holmes and Jason Bard Yarmosky arrived hand-in-hand at the American Ballet Theatre Fall Gala.

The actor was was all minimalist elegance in a slate-green jersey dress with a rounded neckline, long sleeves, and a draped floor-length skirt. The piece hails from It girl-approved New York label Kallmeyer.

The actor and the artist began dating earlier this year, and in the past several months, have attended many a public event together, always looking smitten in coordinating fits.

Last night’s gala honored Cynthia Erivo. The Wicked star was celebrated “for her remarkable contributions to the performing arts and her extraordinary capacity to inspire through multidisciplinary storytelling,” the ABT said. #6779. Ben Ahlers Teases Jack and Bridget’s “Tangible, Objective Obstacle” in The Gilded Age Season 4

Does entering his gentleman era mean leaving her behind?

The rise of Jack Trotter—or as the internet calls him, “Clock Twink”—has been one of The Gilded Age’s most rewarding storylines. He started as a humble footman in the Van Rhijn household and got rich practically overnight after inventing an affordable alarm clock—all while somehow avoiding becoming a terrible person. He is one of the easiest characters on the show to root for. Jack could do practically anything onscreen and I would be thrilled.

His will-they-won’t-they relationship with Bridget, one of the Van Rhijn household’s maids, is part of that. Fans have been hoping for these two to get together for multiple seasons now. But after a close call last season, they seem further apart than ever. The actor behind Jack, Ben Ahlers, chatted with Cosmopolitan about what season 4 has in store for this maybe-couple and whether Jack will be able to stay true to himself as he climbs the social ladder.

What's so interesting about this whole world is the public and private self, and Jack has become a master of that. He has a private self downstairs in the Van Rhijn household as the trickster, the one who can poke at Armstrong or keep the vibe going when everybody's having a long day. My accent would change between the two, upstairs and downstairs, over the last few seasons. But now he's in a completely different scenario where he doesn't have that family. He doesn't have the private self. As he's stepping into these new worlds with inherently manipulative, distrustful, power-hungry people, there's a level of caution.

You don't become a gentleman overnight. You don't make 300 grand and just become a swaggy dude. There's an acclimating that has to happen, and I really enjoyed playing with that tension. A lot of what you see of him over this season is him trying to find the people and the places where he can reconnect with that beautiful, loving private self.

I think he has an opportunity to more so than anybody because he is carving his own path. He was an orphan who grew up in servitude and then elevated into the upper class. There's no one else who has that track. He has the money and the skill set to provide a life for himself. But he doesn't have the support of Banister patting him on the back to tell him to keep going. It’s going to be a challenge. This isolation and new beginning is a really incredible curriculum for the type of man that I know he can be.

Jack and Bridget have one of the most foundational relationships in his life. It's been a bit nebulous. They were kind of romantic, and then they were friends, and then there are glimmers of maybe something happening at the end of season 3. It's a really unique but relatable challenge that when you're in an interpersonal relationship with that much love, how do you foster change in one another, necessary growth when that doesn't always align? Now they're in two separate classes, and that's going to create a tangible, objective obstacle. And I'm really hopeful for them to find that sense of intimacy and connection, regardless of the sort of stations of their lives.

It's so fun to play in that tension, especially with a great scene partner, because you get to try out different tactics and you get to try it at a different event or a party, and sometimes you connect and then it breaks away again. That's what makes people gravitate towards stories like this, because that expansion contraction internally and interpersonally is the whole bag that all of us are going through every day of our lives.

#6777. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#6769. Ahead of Season 4, The Gilded Age Cast Celebrated in New York and L.A.

Ahead of The Gilded Age’s return to screens on November 1, celebrations on each coast saw the cast reunite for a red carpet outing at The Plaza and an elegant bash at Greystone Mansion in Beverly Hills.

At this week’s lavish season 4 premiere party at The Plaza, Carrie Coon, Cynthia Nixon, Audra McDonald, and their fellow stars left their bustles behind. After three seasons of elaborate period costumes, you’d think slipping into a corset every day would make dressing up for a night out a breeze. Alas, think again.

“It’s so much harder to get ready for a red carpet than it is for work,” Coon, clad in an Acne Studios gown, told Vogue. “My dresser, Amber, helps me get into my costumes and cinch my waist from a 28 to a 24. It takes about six minutes and 30 seconds to take off my costumes and wigs. Red carpets are way more stressful, and sometimes the clothes are just as challenging. For me, it’s the shoes. When you get to 16 hours in three-inch heels, you start to lose your mind!”

For the glamorous premiere and party, Nixon opted for a floor-length white fringe dress by Edeline Lee, while Louisa Jacobson stepped out in a cropped Conner Ives blazer and high-waisted trousers. Kelley Curran embraced a softer palette in a mint set by Lein. Morgan Spector gave formalwear an avant-garde twist in an all-white Maison Margiela look, complete with dramatic coattails. Denée Benton channeled Cher in a 1980s-era Bob Mackie gown, McDonald brought the drama in a sheer Bibhu Mohapatra gown, and Ben Ahlers embraced graphic prints in an optic-patterned Kenzo look.

The Plaza’s storied Grand Ballroom, with its glittering crystal chandeliers and intricate gold detailing, provided a suitably opulent setting for the occasion. Hundreds of hydrangeas and lisianthuses adorned the space, while a dramatic centerpiece of oak foliage anchored the room. And then there was the dessert spread: a decadent affair in its own right, featuring cakes, mousses, spiced chai apple crumble, and fondant top hats.

With season 4 premiering Sunday, November 1, the cast offered hints of the social warfare to come. The eight-episode season picks up roughly six months after season 3, with Bertha Russell (Coon) determined to salvage her fractured marriage to railroad tycoon George Russell (Spector)“Everyone is very invested in what’s going to happen between Bertha and George. I can say, rest assured, Bertha never gives up,” Coon teased. “But that doesn’t mean she always wins. Scary, right?”

Meanwhile, Agnes van Rhijn (Christine Baranski) hopes to reclaim her influence among Manhattan’s elite by hosting a dinner for President Grover Cleveland. Bertha counters with a grander affair to advance George’s business interests, setting up another showdown between old money and new. “It’s really fun to spar with her,” Coon said of Baranski. “I respect and admire Christine so much. Her timing is impeccable. You’ll see her character experience an incredible change of fortune, so she’s a bit unhinged.”

Agnes’s sister Ada Forte (Nixon) is stepping into a more independent chapter, trading her role as the family’s steadying influence for a willingness to take risks. She’s now “walking a tightrope and playing with fire,” Nixon said. “I thought, wow, are we really going to go there? I guess we are!”

Love remains complicated for Marian Brook (Jacobson) and Larry Russell (Harry Richardson). Their engagement is over, but feelings remain. “There’s deeper heartbreak than before,” Richardson said. “Larry desperately wants to be with her, but there are so many societal pressures keeping them apart. He’s stumbling and fumbling, and I think that’s quite relatable.”

Marian’s perspective, however, reflects the restrictive social codes of her era. “The expectations for women in 1886, particularly around sexuality and purity, were very, very different,” Jacobson said. “I knew that audiences would have a strong reaction to Marian’s actions, but it made perfect sense to me when I did the research.”

Not every relationship is built on love. Last season, Oscar van Rhijn (Blake Ritson) proposed a marriage of convenience to the newly widowed Enid Winterton (Curran), trading connections and status for her money. Their union promises to challenge society’s conventions further in season 4, fitting for a character who has never been afraid to ruffle feathers. “I’ve been called a hussy in the grocery store!” Curran said with a laugh.

Fan favorite Jack Trotter (Ahlers) is adjusting to life among Manhattan’s elite after selling his clock invention. Having once served the very society he now moves among, Jack must learn its rules. “I had to learn the proper etiquette myself,” Ahlers said. “You see me stepping into this character and Jack stepping into his life in tandem.”

Beyond Manhattan’s old-money dynasties, The Gilded Age shines a light on a thriving Black elite whose stories have rarely been told on television. McDonald, who plays society matriarch Dorothy Scott, is proud that the series showcases more than suffering. “We didn’t just go from slavery to Jim Crow to the ’60s. We’re showing Black excellence, Black joy, and Black love.”

Benton, who plays Peggy Scott, has championed this representation from the start. “It’s been the honor of my career to advocate for that storyline,” she said. “It feels like righting a collective gaslighting in history to really bring the spotlight to people who deserve dignity.”

For one evening at The Plaza, the cast traded their fictional rivalries for a well-deserved moment in the spotlight. But if the series has taught us anything, it’s that no social gathering is complete without a little ambition, a dash of scandal, and a spectacular outfit. After all, on The Gilded Age, the drama never goes out of style. —Paul Chi

Out west, The Gilded Age Cast marked the upcoming season with an outdoor screening at the legendary Greystone Mansion in Beverly Hills.

Commissioned by an oil tycoon in the 1920s, the sprawling Tudor-style estate spans 16 acres of formal English gardens, but this week, all the floral arrangements worth noting were inside the Great Hall.

Upon arrival, over 250 guests were led through Fleurs de Villes’ opulent botanical exhibit inspired by the period drama—which is open to the public through November 4—before congregating on the terrace for a breathtaking view of the city.

While sipping on themed cocktails by the stone balcony, the show’s familiar faces Taissa Farmiga, Kelly Curran, Harry Richardson, and Ben Ahers told Vogue they couldn’t imagine a better spot to celebrate the season premiere in their own backyard.

“It's strange to be so far from New York City,” says Farmiga, who plays heiress-turned-duchess Gladys Russell—daughter of Berta and George. “It makes me think, what would it be like if Gladys would have taken one of Daddy Russell’s trains to come out here?”

After a Champagne toast on the terrace, the cast led guests back up to the gardens to preview the show, marking the first-ever outdoor screening hosted at Greystone. “It’s a piece of history,” said Curran, dressed in a plunging sequined Edeline Lee number befitting the gilded cocktail dress code. “There’s something about the sumptuousness of this show, and to see this in the middle of Los Angeles is so incredibly meta-theatrical.” —Emily Tannenbaum

#6765. Every New Balance 990, Ranked

The New Balance 990 is one of the greatest sneaker lines ever. First released in 1982, the original took four years to develop and cost around $100—a lot for a running shoe back then. But the Boston brand wanted to make the best one it possibly could, and it wasn't cutting corners. More than four decades later, the series has given us some of its best trainers, with each generation making its own adjustments to the suede, the mesh, and everything underfoot.

And with the seventh-gen 990v7 coming in hot, it felt like a good time to revisit the whole thing. A lot has changed since the OG, and some updates have worked better than others. We took stock—from the early pairs to the latest model, from the one that didn't quite hit to the GOAT—and here's every New Balance 990, ranked.

Well, someone had to come in last place. The 990v5 simplified the paneling and added Ortholite insoles, but visually, it's the one we get least hyped up about. The plastic detailing feels a little awkward, and the cleaner upper loses some of the aura of its predecessors. Of course, it's still a good-looking sneaker—there are just six others we'd pick first.

Putting the OG this low feels a little blasphemous, but being first doesn't automatically mean best. The 990v1's slim sole, big “N” logo, and straightforward suede panelling make it an easy pair to wear. It's a handsome thing. We just prefer the more complicated, slightly nerdier designs that NB came up with afterwards.

The newest 990 gets a provisional fifth. There's plenty to like in the reworked upper, including the leather accents, but the busy sole takes some getting used to. From the teaser photos, the proportions don't look quite as convincing as the pairs above it. With the release still ahead, we're judging the design here. A few good colorways could easily move it up.

The 990v6 made a fairly big change: out went the mid-foot saddle, leaving the “N” sitting directly on the mesh, and in with FuelCell cushioning underneath. The result looks longer and more substantial than earlier versions. That's part of the appeal, especially under wider trousers. But all that sole can dominate a fit, and we'd take the slightly neater proportions of our top three.

The 990v2 is the freaky-looking one, and we mean that in the best way possible. The small “N,” angular panels, and sculpted sole give it a very different feel from the rest of the family. There's a lot going on, but the slimmer upper keeps it from looking heavy. It's the pair we'd choose for someone who already has the obvious New Balances and wants something a little more particular.

The 990v4 is probably the one we'd recommend most often. The shape is beefy without being bulky. The suede overlays leave just enough mesh showing through. It works with jeans, loose chinos, and trousers you'd usually save for the office. Nothing feels oversized or awkward. Second place comes down to a small preference: we like the winner's extra detail a little more.

The diamond-patterned tongue. The ribbed “N.” The way the suede panels spread across the mesh. The 990v3 gives you plenty to look at, and somehow it all works together. It has the most convincing proportions of the seven: broad enough to suit loose trousers, with a sole that doesn't completely overwhelm the upper. Even in the standard grey color combo, there's nothing we'd want to change. Our GOAT.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#6763. Lymphatic Drainage: 4 Simple Habits to Add to Your Daily Routine

Lymphatic drainage has become something of a wellness obsession. From sculpting facial massages to body brushes and compression boots, there are seemingly endless ways to coax away puffiness and encourage a more contoured appearance. But the benefits of a healthy lymphatic system extend well beyond what we can see in the mirror.

The lymphatic system is sometimes described as the “Cinderella of medicine”: “underestimated, yet performing a large part of the essential work,” writes cardiothoracic surgeon Dr. Gerald M. Lemole in his book Lymph & Longevity: The Untapped Secret to Health. While its aesthetic benefits have helped bring lymphatic drainage into the mainstream, this intricate network of vessels, nodes, and fluid is fundamental to everything from immune function to maintaining the body's fluid balance.

And, as it turns out, keeping it functioning properly doesn’t necessarily require a standing appointment with your facialist.

Think of the lymphatic system as the body’s behind-the-scenes maintenance crew. Working alongside the circulatory and immune systems, it helps collect excess fluid from tissues, transports immune cells, and carries certain waste products away from cells.

“Lymph transports various substances—large proteins such as antibodies and enzymes, as well as immune system cells that nourish, protect, and coordinate the body—while removing cellular waste through its collecting ducts,” explains Lemole. He likens the system to a “secret river of health,” a network of vessels through which fluid is continuously transported and returned to the bloodstream. Healthy lymphatic function is essential for maintaining fluid balance and supporting the body’s defenses.

But unlike the cardiovascular system, which relies on the heart to pump blood, the lymphatic system has no central pump of its own. Instead, lymph moves through a combination of muscle contractions, breathing, and the action of valves within lymphatic vessels. “You are the pump,” says Ada Ooi, a traditional Chinese medicine specialist, integrative medicine practitioner, and founder of London’s 001 London Longevity Clinic.

The takeaway? Everyday movement, breathing, and other simple lifestyle habits can help support the body's natural lymphatic circulation. Here are four ways to work them into your routine.

One of the simplest ways to support lymphatic circulation requires nothing more than a few minutes of your time. Deep diaphragmatic breathing—the kind that expands your abdomen rather than lifting your shoulders—creates changes in pressure within the chest and abdomen that help move lymphatic fluid through the body.

“The largest lymphatic vessels and the body's main lymphatic ducts are located in the chest cavity,” explains Lemole. “Every deep breath helps circulate the lymph.”

Whether it’s a few minutes of breathwork upon waking, a gentle yoga session, or simply taking a moment to slow your breathing between meetings, the idea is to make deeper, more deliberate breaths part of your day.

Lemole suggests spending a few minutes each morning and evening practicing slow, diaphragmatic breathing. As a bonus, it can also help counter the shallow, hurried breathing that often accompanies stress.

There’s another reason to get up from your desk. Movement is one of the body’s natural mechanisms for transporting lymph, with each muscle contraction helping to propel fluid through the lymphatic vessels.

And it doesn’t have to involve a strenuous workout. A brisk walk, a few flights of stairs, calf raises, squats, or even ankle rotations can all get your muscles working, according to Cecily Braden, esthetician and creator of The Lymphatic Brush.

For those looking to make their routine a little more playful, there’s rebounding: the mini-trampoline workout that’s found fans in Gwyneth Paltrow, Eva Longoria, and Miranda Kerr. The repeated bouncing combines muscle contractions with changes in gravitational force. Lemole advocates for rebounding as a way to encourage fluid movement, and early NASA research explored the physiological effects of trampoline exercise, although evidence that it offers superior lymphatic benefits to other forms of movement remains limited. Still, bouncing for a few minutes in the morning feels like, if anything, an easy bit of movement and a quick mood-booster. And if trampolines aren’t your thing? Walking, dancing, stretching, and other regular physical activities are equally sensible places to start.

If your morning routine could use a gentler start, consider qigong. The centuries-old Chinese practice combines slow, flowing movements with controlled breathing and focused attention, making it an appealing alternative to more vigorous exercise.

For Ooi, qigong naturally brings together several mechanisms involved in lymphatic circulation. “Slow, rhythmic movements of the limbs, deep and coordinated breathing, gentle rotations of the spine and torso that compress and then release the abdomen, and repeated movements of the arms above the heart… Qigong brings together all the elements that promote lymphatic circulation,” she explains.

The combination of gentle muscle activation, diaphragmatic breathing, and torso movements may help encourage lymphatic flow, while the meditative pace offers an opportunity to unwind. Specific evidence for qigong’s effects on lymphatic circulation is still limited, but its broader benefits as a form of mindful movement make it a worthwhile addition to a daily routine.

And there’s no need to master an elaborate sequence. “Two or three movements performed slowly for ten minutes will be far more beneficial to you than a 20 minute sequence done at full speed,” says Ooi.

What you eat and drink matters, too. Staying adequately hydrated supports the body's normal fluid balance, including the production and circulation of lymphatic fluid.

Beyond drinking enough water, there’s growing interest in the potential role of polyphenols—plant compounds known for their antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties—in supporting lymphatic health. Found naturally in berries, beans, vegetables, tea, and a variety of other plant foods, certain polyphenols are being investigated for their effects on lymphatic function. Some have been described as lymphagogues, or substances that may encourage lymph flow, although research in humans remains preliminary.

Rather than reaching for another supplement, the simplest approach is to eat a varied, plant-rich diet and stay well-hydrated. Ultimately, looking after your lymphatic system needn’t become another complicated wellness undertaking. A few deeper breaths, a little more movement, and a balanced diet are all practical ways to support a system that’s already working hard on your behalf.

#6758. 13 Books That Changed Comedian, Author, and Drag King Murray Hill’s Life

Hollywood is a strange and mysterious place, even for those who have operated within its orbit for decades. But author, comedian, and drag king Murray Hill’s new memoir, Showbiz!: My Life as a Middle-Aged Man (Gallery Books), sheds an alternately moving and hilarious light on “the industry,” charting Hill’s rise from a fraught New England childhood to a career starring in New York and Boston cabaret shows and appearing on television series including Somebody, Somewhere, Life & Beth, and Elsbeth.

In the latest edition of Vogue’s Required Reading series, Hill shares a selection of books that have inspired his own memoir and seen him through the ups and downs of Hollywood life.

Kathleen Hanna is a punk-rock icon, feminist trailblazer, and one of my dearest friends. She’s spent her life making noise, breaking rules, and telling the establishment to beat it. What I love about this book is how she writes about the messy, painful, and hilarious realities behind being a revolutionary. She built her own stage, grabbed the microphone, and started a movement. She is my forever inspiration.

Tina Turner didn’t just make a comeback. She kicked down the door in her red patent leather heels and reclaimed her life while inspiring millions of people all over the world. This book is about surviving abuse, and refusing to let somebody else write your ending. I was drawn to performers like Tina because they showed me that no matter how bad things got, you could get out and start where you are. If I were a religious person (I’m still a recovering Catholic), Tina would be my God.

Etta James had one of the greatest voices in music and one helluva life. Addiction, heartbreak, chaos, and that voice...raw, powerful, and breathtaking. I connected with the struggle behind the spotlight. Etta’s story reminded me that surviving is an accomplishment in itself, even before the curtain goes up.

Jeff Hiller is one of my dearest friends, and one of the funniest people I know. He can make me laugh at a drop of a hat (whatever that means). We both spent decades in show business waiting for the phone to ring. Jeff’s story is proof that there’s no expiration date on making it. Especially for us middle-aged guys who love to improv! And let me tell you, nobody deserves a standing ovation more than this guy.

Viola Davis tells her story with such raw honesty, it practically jumps off the page and grabs you by the collar. Growing up with trauma, feeling invisible, and then fighting to be seen…I know a little something about that. She didn’t just find herself, she fought like hell to become herself. And now the world gets to revel in her talent and strength.

Sammy could sing, dance, act, do impressions, tell jokes—the ultimate entertainer. But what stayed with me was everything he had to overcome just to stand on that stage. He faced racism and rejection and still managed to dazzle the world. Sammy taught me that showbiz magic is making it look easy when nothing about it is and that talent always transcends.

I love Sedaris’s ability to find the absurdity in everyday life, especially the humiliating parts. He writes about family, being an outsider, and all the ridiculous things people do to one another. He reminds me that sometimes you don’t need to make anything up. Just tell people what happened, because just as life is absurd, it is funny, too.

Richard Pryor was a genius, a revolutionary, and such a truthful entertainer. He is by far my favorite comedian of all time. He took his pain, his childhood, his addictions, and everything society told him to keep quiet about and turned it into comedy. He wasn’t just telling jokes. He was telling the truth, and sometimes the truth was ugly as hell. Pryor taught me that comedy can be dangerous, uncomfortable, and still bring down the house.

Steve Martin spent years developing an act nobody understood. Sound familiar? He worked every lousy gig imaginable, refined every little detail, and kept going until the audience finally caught up. This book captures the loneliness, obsession, and sheer stubbornness it takes to be a comedian. Nobody sees the thousands of nights you bomb before they call you an overnight success.

Tina Fey is smart, hilarious, and knows how to run the show. That’s a dangerous combination in a business where people love telling you to sit down and shut up. I love her honesty about ambition, insecurity, and the absurdity of show business. She’s living proof that you can be the boss, get the laugh, and still have absolutely no idea what you’re doing half the time.

Marc Shaiman is a walking, talking, piano-playing encyclopedia of show business. This guy has worked with everybody, and I mean EVERYBODY…even me! What I love is that underneath all the Broadway glamour and Hollywood success is a kid who just wanted to put on a show. I know that kid. I am that kid, too.

This is my kind of history book! Give me the drunks, the nightclub comics, the vaudevillians, the hustlers, and the people doing three shows a night for 20 bucks and a sandwich. Comedy has always been filled with outsiders and oddballs trying to make a living getting laughs. Reading this felt like discovering my family tree.

Hannah Gadsby did something extraordinary. She questioned the very thing that saved my life: comedy. For years, I turned my pain into punchlines because making people laugh was easier than telling them what really happened. Hannah challenged the idea that we always have to make our suffering entertaining. Sometimes the bravest thing a comedian can do is tell a story without giving the audience a way out.

Leslie Jones is a force of nature. She’s loud, fearless, outrageous, and doesn’t give a flying f*ck what anybody thinks. She spent years grinding away in comedy clubs before getting her big break. I love anybody who’s been told they’re too much and responds by turning up the volume. Leslie didn’t change herself to fit into show business. She made show business make room for Leslie F*cking Jones!

#6754. This Revamped Field Watch Is More Fall-Coded Than Pumpkin Spice

Watch collaborations can sometimes feel they have been randomly cobbled together, two disparate brands fusing to make something wholly uninteresting. This isn’t the case for Huckberry or the Canadian military watchmaker Marathon, which supplied timekeeping equipment to Allied forces in 1941. The natural alignment between brands gave Huckberry the perfect excuse to rummage through the archives and collaborate on the all-new, 41mm CeraShell Navigator, a pilot’s watch whose design traces back to the Steel Navigator developed with Kelly Air Force Base in 1986.

Military and heritage references are already a big part of Huckberry’s in-house brands, particularly Flint and Tinder. For its take on the Navigator, Huckberry focused on the dial, color, and tweaked a few distinct details rather than changing the watch too dramatically.

That starts with Marathon’s matte-black CeraShell case, here paired with an Arctic white dial for the first time on a CeraShell Navigator. The familiar 12- and 24-hour scales remain, while Huckberry swaps the usual red seconds hand for its hunter orange. Branding has also been kept to a minimum, with Huckberry’s tree logo placed on the crown instead of the already slightly crowded dial.

Together, the black case, white dial, and orange second hand simplify the Navigator’s appearance, but doesn’t drown out its classic military timepiece pedigree. The case is made from Marathon’s CeraShell, a proprietary combination of bio-sourced plastic and ceramic powder that helps keep the watch lightweight. It measures 11.5mm thick and is fitted with a sapphire crystal, while tritium tubes provide constant illumination for the hour and minute hands, hour markers, and bezel pip.

Inside the 41mm case is an ETA F06.115 HeavyDrive quartz movement, with water resistance rated to 60 meters. The watch comes with two straps, an olive-green DEFSTAN nylon strap and a coyote-brown HANK strap made using neoprene and Kevlar, extending the military influence beyond the case and dial.

The watch sits in the duality of being both rugged and completely refined, which is, in some ways, a perfect distillation of both brands. The Navigator is available now exclusively through Huckberry’s site.

#6752. Fall 2026’s Biggest Pedicure Trends Are Here—Shop the Shades

From dark reds and chocolate browns to milky pinks and French tips.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fall has officially arrived at our fingertips. First, Dakota Johnson’s “brown sugar espresso” nails brought a warm, creamy brown into the conversation; then Anne Hathaway’s “cherry noir” manicure made a convincing case for the season’s deep, glossy reds. If the autumnal manicures so far are any indication, this season is shaping up to be deliciously moody polish-wise. But what about your toes?

While they may be spending more time tucked inside boots and sneakers than on display in your go-to summer sandals, there’s no need to abandon them. A fresh coat of polish may be hidden from everyone else, but “nothing is going to feel better curled up next to the fireplace or snuggled inside your slippers than freshly painted toes,” celebrity manicurist Stephanie Stone says on behalf of ORLY.

The color trends are particularly good this season. Thea Green MBE, founder of Nails.INC, looks to the richer, more textured feel of fall wardrobes for inspiration, pointing to “deep burgundies, chocolate browns, sophisticated grays, and jewel tones.” However, she also loves the contrast of a barely-there, milky pedicure against heavier knits, leather, and suede. Plus, you should prepare to keep your nails nice and short this season. As Stone notes, “you’re going to want to keep your toenails short and naturally shaped so they don’t keep bumping up against your shoes.”

So while your favorite fall manicures might be getting all the attention, don’t overlook what’s happening below. Ahead, experts share the fall 2026 pedicure trends predicted to take over—and the shades you can shop to try them yourself.

“The color of the moment is a deep vampy red,” says Stone. Green concurs, sharing, “Deep, delicious, almost-black reds are going to be one of the most sophisticated pedicure looks for fall 2026.” The shade is perfect for fall because it’s dark, moody, fashion-forward, and timeless. “I particularly love a rich burgundy on short, softly squared toenails with a beautiful high-gloss finish,” says Green.

“Chocolate brown is absolutely having its moment,” says Green. From deep espressos to lighter caramels, “we’re moving towards beautiful, edible-looking tones.” A chocolate pedicure feels warmer and softer than black, but has the same sophisticated impact.

Not every Fall pedicure needs to be dark. In fact, Green loves a delicate, barely-there pink against chunky knits and dark denim. “Soft, milky pinks are subtle, elegant, and give that lovely ‘your nails but better’ effect,” she says. The key is choosing a shade that looks softly polished rather than overly opaque, with a beautiful, glossy finish. “I love it on shorter, naturally rounded toenails for that perfectly groomed, effortless finish.”

“French tips are always in style, but can make you feel extra chic when worn on your toes in the fall,” says Stone. Since wearing the design on your feet can feel like such a statement, autumn is the perfect time to try it since your pedicure is more for you than anyone else.

“We’re seeing a move towards finishes that are a little more interesting than a traditional crème,” says Green. “Whether that’s chrome, pearl, or iridescent is completely up to you.” Stone makes a strong case for cat eye. “Cat eye nail art is still all the rage, and it makes for a great pedicure design too.” Green adds, “ I absolutely love the idea of a delicate iridescent pedicure for fall. The effect should be almost translucent and beautifully luminous, catching the light as you move.” Any fun finish makes a pedicure feel special without relying on elaborate nail art.

”One of my favorite emerging color directions is what I call the new neutrals: mushroom, greige, taupe and soft, earthy grey,” says Green. These shades are perfect for a minimalist, incredibly chic look. These warmer grey tones make them much more wearable than traditional cold grey shades, particularly for fall. “I love this look with short, neatly shaped toenails and a glossy finish.”

#6749. As Midterms Approach, US Consumer Anxiety Is High — But Fashion Is Set to Rally

Ahead of Tuesday’s midterm elections, US consumer anxiety is at a high.

Thirty-four percent of respondents to an Economist/YouGov survey conducted in October considered the economy and inflation to be the most important issues facing the US. Sixty-seven percent of respondents said they disapproved of President Trump’s handling of inflation. And, when asked how they felt about their futures compared to 10 years ago, 48% said they were more pessimistic.

As reports of consumer sentiment edging close to all-time lows in the weeks leading up to the midterm elections (it dipped once again in October, falling to 48 from 48.1 in September, according to the University of Michigan’s consumer survey), luxury conglomerates continued referring to North America as a “bright spot” in their most recent quarterly earnings, reaffirming the United States as the boon that it has been for luxury this past year.

“People express frustration and anger and disappointment — and then they go out and shop,” says HSBC US head of retail and apparel Eric Fisch, noting that, despite cost of living increases, this applies to shoppers from mass to luxury. “I’ve consistently seen that, especially since Covid, where consumer spending disconnects from consumer sentiment.”

Though analysts have speculated a US normalization following its growth streak, the country has, for now, remained relatively stable. The Americas led growth at Zegna, up 14%. “In particular, the Americas continued its double-digit trend, despite an increasingly demanding comparison base,” executive chair Ermenegildo “Gildo” Zegna said in a statement. At Hermès, Americas revenues were up 8.8% in the third quarter, also posting the highest growth by region. North America was Kering’s only growth region, up 1% in Q3. It was also up for Moncler and Ferragamo.

Analysts aren’t convinced that the forthcoming midterm elections will have much impact on this still-positive trajectory. If anything, they agree, there might be a slight dip, but not one that will hurt the holidays. “Historically speaking, consumers don’t really let elections influence their spending all that much,” says Neil Saunders, managing director of Globaldata’s retail division. Fisch notes that the ramp up in political ads has crowded exposure for brands, but that should clear up by the holidays.

Brands and analysts alike remain bullish on the US. Fisch, who has just returned from two weeks in Europe, says that many of the brands he spoke with on the ground are hedging their bets on the US shopper. The recent unrest in Europe and continued softness in the Middle East means brands are banking even more — and putting extra pressure — on the US. “The events in Europe are only going to provide more of a cloud over the consumer there, making it more challenging,” Fisch says. “The US continues to be the growth engine and the opportunity.” He adds that many brands are continuing to invest capital in the country.

If the midterms impact on consumer spending will be minor, how is the rest of the year shaping up for the American shopper as we approach the high-stakes holiday season?

It’s now widely accepted that consumer sentiment doesn’t necessarily align with what people are doing with their money. “There has been a growing disconnect between how people say they feel and how they act in terms of spending and buying, including on fashion,” Saunders says. “Despite the gloom, consumers have been spending more on apparel.”

This splintering began after the pandemic, and has been referred to as the “vibecession”, wherein people are downbeat about the economy even if they’re doing financially OK. “Sentiment measures mood and, I would argue, empathy. Consumers may feel personally OK or living comfortably because of their wage gains, but feel others cannot be in a good spot or living comfortably,” Christine Barton, managing director and senior partner at BCG, explains. “Consumers can be trusted about themselves and their households; just not good judges about the economy and real consumption.”

This disjuncture partly stems from the fact that caution and uncertainty are now embedded into the consumer psyche, he adds. Because of this, continued anxiety is unlikely to change the trajectory of the luxury or fashion segments. “What will cause a slowdown is if consumers see a tangible and prolonged compression of their incomes,” Fisch says. “We have seen a bit of these things over recent months thanks to high inflation and slower wage growth, but it is not all that pronounced.”

For fashion retailers and brands, this should be encouraging, says Barton. She points to the firm’s recent holiday research, which found that clothing and shoes are the top planned Black Friday purchase category across every generation in the US, with purchase intent increasing across all age groups compared to last year.

Though US consumers are still spending, they’re doing so in a more researched and considered way, analysts flag. “People are thinking more deeply before they buy and are undertaking more activities like price checking — that’s the area where we’re seeing the shift,” Saunders says. Barton points to a recent BCG survey, whereby 91% of consumers actively research their fashion choices before making a purchase and 53% identify quality for the price as the biggest driver of value.

“Consumers are clearly feeling the pressure of higher prices, but that doesn’t necessarily mean they’re pulling back on spending,” says Barton. “What we’re seeing instead is a greater emphasis on perceived value, and making sure purchases feel personally worthwhile.”

This, Fisch flags, is why the advanced contemporary category is doing the best of his portfolio. “The consumer is seeing a lot of value for their money. It’s on trend, it’s great product, it’s great quality, and they’re struggling with the price increases that true luxury has gone through over the past few years,” he explains. “You have to look at those two together and say, ‘Oh, the consumer is really quite strong. It’s just the high luxury priced a lot of people out.’” This is evidenced in the success of brands like TWP and Harris Tapper, which are operating at a price point that’s steep, but well below that of luxury brands. Per BCG’s Center for Consumer Insight (CCI) research, 67% of respondents wouldn’t buy a product, even if they could afford it, unless they feel it offers strong value, Barton says.

This attitude will inform US holiday shopping habits, as seasonal deals boost spending further, analysts expect. Eighty-five percent of consumers plan to shop year-end sales events (like Black Friday) this year, up 2 percentage points from 2025, with clothing and shoes leading purchase intentions across every generation, per BCG. “We saw this for the last several holidays, where pent-up demand in the ‘want’ categories and purchases were delayed into holiday spending,” Barton says. “What matters for brands is understanding that consumers can feel anxious about the future while continuing to spend in the present.”

“The US is going to drive growth as it has for the prior three quarters,” Fisch says of Q4’s holiday season, often the most important quarter for brands. And beyond that, into 2027? “I don’t see anything that would lead me to believe that it won’t be a growth year in the US.”
