#6741. Inside Kering’s First Water Resilience Lab in Italy

This story is part of ‘The Business Case for Nature’, a series exploring fashion’s impact on nature and vice versa. Read more here.

In 1966, the banks of the Arno river burst, causing the most destructive flood Florence had ever experienced. It took dozens of lives, displaced thousands of people, and claimed millions of rare books and art works.

The region has seen many more floods since then. In 2023, Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni declared a state of emergency for the worst-hit areas amid Storm Ciarán, which caused an estimated €1.89 billion in damages. One of the fashion suppliers hit was Prato-based Beste Group, whose HQ sits directly on the bank of the Bisenzio river. The company purchased a new machine — worth around €7 million — the month prior, which was then destroyed by the floods. It was only insured for 60% of the price, so replacing it cost the company several million euros. On top of that, the waist-high water brought production to a standstill for three weeks.

When Rachel Kolbe Semhoun, head of sustainable sourcing and nature initiatives at Kering, read about these losses in Vogue Business, it started a chain reaction that eventually led her to create the group’s industry-first water strategy. “They had done everything right inside the factory, but they were still flooded,” she says. “It was a great example of how nobody can make resilience happen by themselves. The only thing that would have helped was collectively investing in better nature infrastructure upriver.”

The luxury conglomerate has outlined a plan to become “water positive”, laying the groundwork for other brands to follow. It’s a bold move, but experts say it’s just the beginning.

When Kering set that strategy 18 months ago, its goal was to become “net water-positive” by 2050, with “measured net-positive water impacts” in 10 priority water basins by 2035. It was a bold aim. No other fashion company had developed a dedicated strategy for water resilience, and there was no blueprint for the French luxury group to follow.

On Monday, Kering officially opened its first Water Resilience Lab in the Arno Basin, outlining a roadmap for building water resilience that it says can be replicated in the other nine priority areas, spanning France, Spain, Türkiye, Mongolia, India, Peru, Argentina, South Africa and Australia. Vogue Business got a first look.

“Most of fashion’s strategic raw materials rely on water,” says Kering chief sustainability and institutional affairs officer, Marie-Claire Daveu. This doesn’t just apply to the growing phase for natural fibers like wool, leather and cotton, but also to the production processes that follow, including washing, dyeing and tanning. “To ensure resilience and business continuity for the fashion industry, we have to support the regeneration and replenishment of the key basins where we operate as an industry, which are home to our suppliers. Ultimately, this becomes a significant driver of long-term value creation for businesses, strengthening supply chain resilience, reducing water-related risks, and supporting the health of ecosystems and communities our industry relies on.”

Kering started with the Arno Basin because of the “irreplaceable know-how” specific to the region, as well as the many local, strategic suppliers it relies on for everything from ready-to-wear and leather goods to accessories and jewelry. “The Arno Basin offers a perfect opportunity to create and test this new model,” says Daveu, “because it has the governance and the stakeholders that can carry through the lab’s ambitious approach.”

That ambitious approach involves bringing together public institutions, businesses, academia, the scientific community and financial actors. In the case of the Arno Basin, that includes Kering’s Regenerative Fund for Nature co-founder Conservation International, environmental consultancy Etifor, the University of Florence and multiple layers of local government, as well as the Northern Apennines River Basin Authority and local land reclamation authorities ANBI Toscana and ANCI Toscana. LVMH and Burberry have also joined the efforts. “It’s been very important for the lab to build on the efforts, understanding and achievements by public authorities in the region,” says Daveu. “The lab is amplifying what has previously been done, while filling gaps and creating new models.”

Kering has officially launched its first water resilience lab, in Italy’s Arno Basin.

There are three main pillars to the roadmap: creating robust and innovative governance structures that allow all stakeholder groups to be heard; creating a financial model that incentivizes change without relying too much on philanthropy; and building out a portfolio of nature-based and industrial projects for various stakeholders to implement, each of which has a strong business case.

Future climate and nature risks are central to that. By 2030, Tuscany is expected to see upward of 50 days of heat stress each year, leaving local communities vulnerable to water scarcity and other water-related risks. But, according to Kering, if 48 million cubic meters of water can be retained in the Arno Basin, the impact of future flooding events will be drastically reduced, with each cubic metre of water retained avoiding approximately €157 in flood damage.

The lab has identified six key intervention points in the local area, where it says investments can generate the greatest impact: Villa Petriolo, Ombrone/Bisenzio, Altopascio/Lake Sibolla, Pisa/Era Basin, Usciana/Pescia River, and Arezzo/Chiana Valley. In each location, “multi-sectorial and private-public consensus has been a real game-changer”, says Daveu.

The roadmap was based on leading methodologies, including volumetric water benefit accounting (VWBA), which allows companies to quantify water stewardship impacts in consistent, comparable units, and the Science-based Targets for Nature (SBTN) of which Kering was an early adopter. These approaches were supplemented by a basin-specific methodology built by the University of Florence, which provides the scientific basis for quantifying risks and outcomes.

It’s a robust start, but some fine-tuning is needed to turn this into a reliable and replicable model other brands can lean on, says Daveu. “A key limitation is the lack of recognition of the role nature-based solutions play in mitigating risk and building long-lasting resilience,” she explains. “This limitation is part of the work the lab will address; innovating the governance and the financial model to go beyond philanthropy to make nature-based resilience a viable investment.”

Moving forward, proving the return on investment for water resilience initiatives like this will be critical to long-term success, she adds. Until now, investments in water resilience have largely been funded by philanthropic foundations, and projects have developed in siloes, minimizing the potential for impact. “Philanthropy will never be enough to support the needed investments for real resilience,” says Daveu. “Efforts need to be coordinated to address water risks in a holistic way [...] and become truly resilient. Amplifying impacts and investments is necessary to have a viable financing model for nature to bring investors to the table.”

#6739. Calvin Klein’s Hot Streak Continues With Jude Bellingham Fronting Its Iconic Underwear Campaign (Exclusive)

Of course, they’d tap football’s soccer’s finest.

Calvin Klein’s iconic underwear ads have been a hot topic since the ’80s, as some of Hollywood’s hunkiest guys fronted the campaigns on blown-up billboards. Michael B. Jordan, Justin Bieber, Jeremy Allen White, and Bad Bunny have been making the girls, gays, and theys go feral over the years, and its latest campaign star is no different. Now, the legendary fashion brand has met its match in global football star Jude Bellingham.

Aside from being one of the best soccer players in the world (and his friendship with fellow footballer Erling Haaland), the 23-year-old athlete is known for two things: his chiseled bone structure and killer abs. So it’s no wonder CK tapped him as the face of its Icon Cotton Stretch underwear—and Cosmopolitan to share some steamy behind-the-scenes pics from the shoot, lensed by Oliver Hadlee Pearch in Madrid last summer.

“I really enjoyed working on this campaign,” Jude said in a statement sent to Cosmo. “I liked the idea of ‘the icon underneath’—that confidence starts with feeling comfortable in yourself. Calvin Klein is such an iconic brand, so it was great to bring that idea to life in my own way.”

Before Jude’s debut CK campaign was officially revealed on November 2, the brand teased the collaboration with a billboard in one of its “out-of-home” placements in New York City. The photo, which was blown up in SoHo, featured Jude’s signature muscles without showing his face for a full-circle IYKYK moment.

It didn’t take long for Jude’s dedicated fan base to recognize the body that brought him all his accomplishments on the field. The brand also dropped a similar teaser to Instagram on October 27, writing, “The next icon. Coming soon.”

Everyone say, “Thank you, Calvin Klein,” for blessing us with these new pics of Jude!

#6737. The Best Perfumes for an Effortlessly Chic French-Girl Allure

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Discover the irresistible Parisian-inspired fragrances worth making room for on your vanity.

There’s something undeniably captivating about the effortless elegance of French style: barely-there makeup; perfectly undone hair; and impeccably curated outfits that somehow look as though they were thrown together in minutes. As French-girl-inspired looks continued to fill our feeds, we’ve found ourselves wondering how else to channel that same carefree confidence and understated sophistication. The answer, it turns out, may be as simple as hunting down the right fragrance.

French women are famously devoted to perfume, often treating a spritz of their signature scent as a non-negotiable part of their morning routine. But what exactly makes a fragrance feel so quintessentially French? According to Natalie Cetto, perfumer at iconic Parisian fragrance house Diptyque, softer, fresher notes are the key. “Fragrances associated with a certain Parisian aesthetic are often built around a subtle balance of timeless elegance, spontaneous freshness, and understated sensuality; that effortless effect that never feels overly composed, yet, in fact highly crafted,” she explains.

According to Cetto, florals and citrus notes are a good place to start. “This can be expressed through luminous notes such as bergamot or neroli, paired with flowers like rose, jasmine, or iris, treated with transparency. The base often relies on musks, cedar, or sandalwood to create a softer, skin-like trail. Whatever the palette used, the idea is to preserve the natural quality of the raw materials while seeking true sophistication: allowing the nobility of the ingredients to shine through, without excess or heaviness.”

Simply put, the best French-girl fragrances are sophisticated without feeling overpowering, leaving behind a subtle yet memorable impression. Drawing on Cetto’s insights and recommendations from Bettina O’Neill, Scentbird’s EVP of business development and merchandising, we’ve pinpointed the perfumes that best capture that effortlessly chic Parisian sensibility. We also asked French makeup artist and Violette_FR founder Violette Serrat for her top picks, and rounded out the list with evocative scents that stood out in our own research and testing. Ahead, discover the most alluring scents worthy of a place on your vanity.

Not just a pretty bottle, Lancôme’s Trésor has a storied history in French perfumery. First introduced in 1952 with a grand celebration at Paris’ Palais de Chaillot, the original fragrance was considered a jewel of its time, housed in a diamond-inspired bottle with 75 hand-faceted sides. More than seven decades later, the fruity-floral perfume remains a timeless icon and the epitome of French-girl elegance.

Notes: Rose, lilac, peach, apricot blossom

Consider this the ultimate date-night potion. Crafted to evoke a stroll through the heart of Paris after dark, it couldn’t be more French-girl coded. Musky patchouli lends a sensual depth, while vanilla adds an irresistible creaminess that makes it all the more alluring.

Notes: Aldehydes, iris, patchouli, musks, ambergris accord, vanilla

Henry Rose’s French Exit is a truly intoxicating warm floral with an understated elegance that makes it an ideal signature scent for everyday wear. Lush blackberry lends a hint of sweetness before giving way to a sensual, musky drydown.

Notes: Blackcurrant bud, pink pepper, tuberose, jasmine sambac

“For a quintessentially French perfume, Orphéon plays with polished woods, musks, and floral facets to evoke a more urban and intimate elegance.”—Natalie Cetto, perfumer at Diptyque

“Lilyphéa brings something airier and more spontaneous, with vegetal, fresh, and slightly creamy notes. There is the idea of discreet sophistication, texture and the nobility of the ingredients.”—Natalie Cetto, perfumer at Diptyque

Prada’s Paradoxe fragrance family has grown into quite the collection, from the original floral scent to the gourmand-inspired Radical Essence. Its latest addition, Paradoxe Sweet Chemistry, launched earlier this year and is a sweet scent lover’s dream. Upon the first spritz, you’re greeted by captivating florals like jasmine and orange blossom, balanced by a burst of juicy tangerine and bright bergamot that keeps the sweetness from becoming overpowering.

Notes: Bergamot, tangerine, pear, neroli, jasmine, orange blossom, amber, white musk, bourbon, vanilla

“I might be biased, but Avec Amour is genuinely my go-to. It’s infused with airy notes of bergamot and ylang-ylang, with a warm, earthy base of musky amber, sandalwood, and vetiver. Smelling it is almost like a little ritual; it’s comforting yet still sensual. And it’s so easy to layer. On vacation, I’ll wear it with Kiehl’s Musk Body Lotion. The two work together so beautifully, bringing out that soft, musky, skin-like quality.”–Violette Serrat, founder of Violette_FR

Notes: Amber, santal, coconut water, vetiver, ylang-ylang, bergamot

First, a moment for the gorgeous stiletto-shaped bottle that houses the Good Girl fragrance collection—so chic! Inside, you’ll find a bold blend of black cherry and rose, combining the sweetness of the florals and richness of the fruit to create something moody and romantic. Just one spritz is enough to keep you smelling divine from day to night.

Notes: Black Cherry, red rose, vanilla, tuberose, bitter almond, cocoa

“This is a modern floral that opens with juicy lychee, peach, and bergamot before revealing a delicate bouquet of peony, jasmine, and orange blossom. Its balance of fruity brightness and soft sensuality makes it a distinctive expression of contemporary Parisian elegance.”—Bettina O’Neill, EVP of business development and merchandising at Scentbird

Notes: Bergamot, lychee, peach, jasmine, peony, orange blossom, transparent wood, moss, musk

“Gris Chanel is a sensual, woody scent. Its soft, spicy warmth and understated sophistication offer a modern, genderless take on Parisian sensuality.”—Bettina O’Neill, EVP of business development and merchandising at Scentbird

Notes: Black tea, fig, cardamom, iris, bourbon vetiver, sandalwood, tonka bean

“I tend to gravitate toward fragrances that feel nuanced and a little unexpected, but still fresh. One brand I’ve been loving the past few years is D’orsay. Each scent has an intimate, layered quality, and I find myself reaching for Tonka Hysteria again and again. The tonka brings this beautiful, addictive warmth without feeling like a typical sweet perfume. There’s something quietly mysterious about it.”—Violette Serrat, founder of Violette_FR

Notes: Mandarin, pink pepper, olibanum, cinnamon, iris butter, iris resin, Lily of the Valley, tonka Bean, benzoin, vanilla

“Inspired by a tuberose field at night, this lush, expressive floral centers around tuberose and [complements] its ginger and a juicy green pear accords. It especially stands out for its emphasis on high-quality raw materials.”—Bettina O’Neill, EVP of business development and merchandising at Scentbird

Notes: Ginger, green pear, tuberose absolute and enfleurage, green tea leaf

“Featuring iris, patchouli, and vanilla, this sophisticated scent blends sweetness and elegance, making it a timeless, versatile fragrance that feels both comforting and luxurious.”—Bettina O’Neill, EVP of business development and merchandising at Scentbird

“YSL Libre offers an unexpected contrast of traditionally masculine and feminine notes that give it a modern, confident edge indicative of effortless French chic.”—Bettina O’Neill, EVP of business development and merchandising at Scentbird

Notes: Lavender essence, orange blossom, musk accord

When trying to evoke a romantic, effortless French attitude, it is often better to avoid accords that feel overly demonstrative or saturated. Very sweet gourmand facets, excessively powerful woods, or certain overly “juicy” fruits can quickly create an impression that feels more constructed than natural. The idea is instead to preserve transparency and contrast, with compositions that remain nuanced, textured, and close to the skin. Even when a fragrance is sensual or distinctive, it still maintains a sense of restraint.

#6736. Confessions of a Pants Girl: How I Finally Came to Love the Skirt

I’ve always been a pants girl. Jeans, low-waisted trousers, oversized sweats—I’ve always gravitated towards pieces you can throw on without overthinking and still feel like yourself. Skirts have never really been off the table, but they’ve only ever played a supporting role in my wardrobe.

That might be about to change. Following the latest shows, I’ve found myself flirting with skirts a little harder. Prada’s spring 2027 show, built around the idea of the skirt—63 of them, to be exact—planted the first seeds in my mind. Green shoots then bloomed after seeing Matthieu Blazy’s spring 2027 collection for Chanel. “For me, the skirt is like a T-shirt. I put all my ideas there,” Mrs. Prada famously stated in a 2006 interview.

Both shows got me thinking: could I, a devoted pants girl, genuinely make the switch? If Miuccia Prada sees the skirt as an everyday essential, perhaps I should start treating it like one, too. The trick—for me at least—is to style a skirt the way I would my favorite pair of trousers. Nothing too precious, nothing that feels overly put together. The skirt doesn’t have to be pencil thin; form-fitting and high on the waist. And it doesn’t necessarily need to be worn with heels either.

As Blazy and Mrs. Prada prove, a skirt can sit on the lower waist, or come in a stretch poplin that’s easy for me to bike in. It can have side pockets—to hold my phone or a pacifier—and be belted, because why not? Contrast is key to making the skirt feel more like me. I want that same nonchalant feel I get from wearing jeans—just with a different silhouette. Borrowing from menswear codes helps to keep the look relaxed and resist the urge to make the skirt the entire statement.

I know won’t be giving up pants altogether—but for now, I’m certainly more open to the idea of giving skirts a little more room in my wardrobe. Here’s how I’m styling spring’s skirt trends for right now.

I’m all about contrast when it comes to styling—it creates more excitement and personality—and here I paired a knit Chanel skirt with a Supreme polo for a more casual feel. A pop of red brings the look home.

I like mixing materials and dressing down pieces in my wardrobe so they’re not reserved for special occasions. This embellished skirt is a work of art, but pairing it with a simple cashmere knit and loafers gives it a cozier feel. I added a chain belt to bring in a touch of masculinity and create even more contrast.

A leather skirt has year-round potential, styled in the summer with a tucked oversized tee and sandals or during the winter with a chunky knit and boots. The most important thing is getting the fit right. I like mine fitted, so the silhouette speaks for itself. I’ve paired it here with a similarly snug leather jacket, which is exactly how I’ll be wearing for a night out over the next couple of months—warm and snatched at the same time.

This skirt is so comfortable and practical. The poplin stretch means I can bike in it, or sit on the floor and play with my kids. I’ve styled it as I’d wear it on a daily basis, with flat boots and a track jacket, but I could just as easily dress it up for a night out with glossy heels and a little tank top. I love pieces that can be worn in multiple ways.

#6732. Engagement Ring Care: 13 Tips From the Experts

Engagement ring care requires taking a few precautions to ensure the longevity of your jewelry piece. Using proper cleaning techniques and avoiding wearing the stone in certain conditions can keep it safe and shining.

Newly engaged? It’s no surprise if engagement ring care is one of the first things you typed into your search bar. With a beautiful investment to show off on your finger, you probably want to make sure you can protect this new heirloom and make it shine so brightly that everyone just has to ask you about it. Since this is a jewelry piece you could wear for the rest of your life, it's important to take the proper precautions to ensure it can last for decades to come.

After my proposal, I became lightly obsessive about how to maintain and clean an engagement ring at home and while traveling. I got all the cleaning formulas, was very particular about when I should take it off, and made sure to get insurance right away. While it might have felt like I was doing too much to protect my ring, it has yet to lose a stone, get stolen, or bent out of shape.

Want to know how the pros go about engagement ring care? Ahead, The Clear Cut founder Olivia Landau and Ring Concierge CEO and founder Nicole Wegman break down the essentials you should know to make your ring last. From when to take it off to how to clean your diamond or gemstone, these jewelers give the inside scoop.

Just as you will see a mechanic to make sure your car is in great shape, you should consider seeing a jeweler from time to time to give your engagement ring some TLC. “We recommend getting your ring checked up to make sure all the prongs are still tight, everything is still in place, and get a polish so it sparkles like new,” shares Landau, who is a GIA Graduate Gemologist. You can either work with the jeweler you purchased the ring from to give it a touch-up, or find a local jeweler who may often do it for free or a small fee.

There are going to be quite a few occasions where wearing your engagement ring is not advised. While you don’t want to damage your ring in those instances, you also don’t want to misplace your precious rock. Get a few ring dishes to place around your home to easily have a spot where you know it can stay safe. “I take my jewelry off every day when I get home,” says Wegman. “You can certainly wear it, but if you’re using your hands, doing dishes, or taking care of kids, there's just no reason to have it on and risk there being damage. Also, it gets dirty so easily.”

Heading to your workout class with your engagement ring? It’s best to rethink that move. “Please, please, don’t do this," warns Landau. “Your engagement ring is delicate and it is not indestructible. The metal on your band (especially if you have a thin band) is malleable and can get damaged if you work out. Lifting weights and even cycling can be especially dangerous if you have a pavé, too. Whatever you do, take it off while you exercise.” One solution if you don’t feel like leaving it at home? “A great tip is to put your engagement ring on your necklace for your workout,” she suggests.

Notice your ring losing its luster? It’s probably just a little dirty. “With the oils on our hands, soaps, and other things we come into contact with every day, your ring can start to fade,” warns Landau. Luckily, learning how to clean your engagement ring isn’t too difficult. Her advice for a DIY clean is to take a bowl of soapy warm water and soak the ring for about 10 minutes. After the time is up, take a soft-bristled toothbrush and brush it gently. “It will be shining like new,” she says. “You don’t want to over-clean your ring—especially if you have white gold—but you should give your ring a deep cleaning every two to four weeks.”

While your shower is a great place to get yourself clean, it’s not the best place to take your engagement ring. “Some people say it is safe, but in the shower, your ring is much more likely to slip off and fall down the drain," shares Landau. “It is also more likely to get dirtier quicker because of all the soaps and hair products we use in the shower.”

Is your home in need of a deep clean that might require some harsher chemicals to get the job done? Then it’s best you take off your ring while doing so. Either place your ring safely on a ring dish or put on gloves to protect both your hands and your jewelry from getting in contact with any chemicals. Also consider taking it off while cooking or doing other household chores to avoid extra grime getting stuck in your settings.

When you go to bed, so should your engagement ring. “A lot of people sleep with their ring and think it is totally fine. I recommend having a small dish near your bed and keeping your ring there, instead,” says Landau. “When you sleep with your ring, your fingers can swell and cut off your circulation. You may injure yourself or the ring—all in all, it is better to play it safe.”

“Don’t go swimming in the ocean or in the pool with your engagement ring! I am guilty of this,” admits Landau. “Sometimes when I am wearing my ring, I do go in the pool with chlorine. Often times it is clean after and totally fine, but it is, again, better to play it safe.” While the pool is not the safest place for your engagement ring, consider the ocean a danger zone where you could damage or lose your ring forever. “Please, please do not wear your ring in the ocean! The salty water will make your ring really dirty-looking. And God forbid if it falls off—that is everyone’s worst nightmare.”

When choosing your engagement ring band size, it’s a good idea to think about how the width of your finger might change throughout the year or over time. After all, a ring that is too big can easily slip off, while a ring that’s too small can be painful. If you size your ring finger during the summer (when heat tends to make your hands swell), it’s possible that the band will be too big during the winter when exposed to cold. If you get pregnant or lose weight, this can also affect your ring size. It’s a good idea to purchase ring-sizing beads to adjust a ring that’s too big. If it’s too small and you don’t expect your size to change much in the future, consider getting it professionally resized.

Along with preserving your ring physically, it’s also important to have financial peace of mind if your engagement ring gets lost, stolen, or broken. “We recommend insuring your engagement ring,” expresses Landau. “Jewelry insurance can protect you against loss, damage, theft, or mysterious disappearance. You can usually use the insurance appraisal provided by your jeweler to get a quote from an insurer. Many insurance companies can help insure your ring the same day!”

One way to make sure your ring is appraised and insured for a fair price is to ensure that it is GIA certified, which details all the specs of your ring’s quality. It's also a great idea to know where exactly your diamond came from. “The Clear Cut works with De Beers’s Tracr, a blockchain technology that allows clients to trace their diamond’s journey—capturing its story from billions of years ago to the moment it becomes part of theirs,” says Landau. “For pieces as meaningful as engagement rings, knowing where your diamond comes from adds a layer of connection and confidence, making it something to be treasured for generations.”

When traveling with your engagement ring, it’s a good idea to become familiar with your hotel safe. “I put jewelry in the hotel safe all the time,” notes Wegman. “I’ve never had an issue, so I think if you’re traveling, that’s your very best bet.” She also advises storing it away as you’re on your way to any destination. “When I'm traveling from an airport to a hotel or taking public transport, I wouldn't wear anything big because it’s very easy for someone to clock that and then see where you’re going,” the jeweler shares.

However, if you are traveling somewhere that’s particularly high risk for theft, you might want to leave your ring at home. Although some people consider going abroad with a fake travel ring, Wegman says they’re not worth it. “If you’re worried about where you’re going, nobody knows it’s real or fake, so you’re putting yourself at the same risk,” she says. “From an insurance standpoint, you’re better off someone stealing your CZ than your diamond. But to me, the bigger concern is your safety.”

While it’s important to store your ring, don’t do it in a way that can damage your setting. “People forget how soft gold is by nature. So if you have a cute little concrete ring dish, that’s a bad idea. It’s going to scratch the gold,” shares Wegman. “You either want to have some kind of velvet or leather, or a softer hard material like plastic or resin.” She adds, “You could do a really smooth marble or ceramic, but then I wouldn't chuck it in.” While traveling, Wegman also advises having a jewelry case with a hard outer shell. “If you're going to shove it in a bag and someone sits on it, it’s gold and it will bend,” says the jeweler.

Different ring settings require different maintenance. If you’re curious about using an ultrasonic cleaner to really get the grime off your ring, you should only do so with a strong setting. “A classic gold solitaire or a bezel, you’re pretty safe,” says Wegman. “Where you want to be careful is with a bunch of micro-pave work. The size of the prongs used to hold down micro-pave diamonds is so tiny that you can actually shake the diamonds loose in an ultrasonic machine.” With those settings, Wegman says to stick to a steam cleaner or old-fashioned cleanser and soft brush.

Have a gemstone instead of a diamond? You might not even want to risk a foam cleaner. While sapphires and rubies are harder stones that can be cleaned like a diamond, an emerald is quite soft. “Instead, you want to get them re-oiled every five to 10 years or so,” advises Wegman. She adds, “Anything with pearls needs to be treated very carefully and cannot go into any kind of cleaner, soap situation, or chlorine.”

Olivia Landau is a GIA Graduate Gemologist and 4th-generation jeweler. She is the founder and CEO of the NYC-based jewelry brand The Clear Cut.

Nicole Wegman is the founder and CEO of Ring Concierge, a digitally native brand redefining how women shop for fine jewelry.

#6728. Why Is It So Hard to Call Someone Out on the Group Chat?

Read the rest of GQ’s special report on Cornell here and here.

The screenshot, circulated in reporting on the allegation of sexual assault by several members of the Chi Phi fraternity at Cornell in 2024, is painful to look at, and painfully familiar: a more extreme example of an episode that plays out on our own phones. Someone in the group chat says something fucked-up. You cringe as laughing emojis roll in. You think about saying something. You probably don’t. And you feel worse for it. Northwestern sociologist Aaron Shaw, who has studied how groups talk to each other online, explains why it happens.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

This kind of interaction is seen linked to the general disinhibition effects documented in digital communications: If your face isn’t there, and if you’re not confronted with the presence of other people, it’s a lot easier to behave in ways that may break with normal social conventions. There’s a long legacy of misogynistic groups existing and thriving. Some people might want to say, “Oh, this is a problem because of Snapchat,” but what research in the last 30 years suggests is that people will find ways to connect no matter what digital communication platform they are using. Research about online communities dates back to the 1980s and ’90s, and some of the high-drama incidents occurring in that time were around rape culture.

Gaming is an incredibly heterogeneous space of human activity, but there are some communities where violations of social norms are not just okay, but important: “If you want to play the game, you’ve got to get over it.”

They might be communicating at multiple levels in ways that are hard to see from the outside. By drawing on particular words they’ve used in the past, they might be signaling things to each other, like their membership in the group, their commitment to each other. Their desire to stay connected with each other may be more powerful than the desire to speak in an empathetic way about, in this case, the experience of a young woman.

Some research on bystander effects suggests that the larger the group size, the less likely any one individual in that group is to speak out against it. Individuals may feel like they don’t have control or that they can’t be responsible for the behavior of everybody in the chat. If their sense is that others in the group think this is okay—or would be willing to ostracize them if they were to speak out—they’re less likely to be willing or able to speak out.

There’s also a performative element. They are putting on a show; they are onstage for each other. Group chats really do serve that purpose. It’s hard to know what elements of that performance are, in a sense, fiction and what elements are a factual representation of the events that night in the frat house. The tropes these young guys may be using may be profoundly objectionable, may be hyper-misogynistic, may undermine the humanity and rights and autonomy of women, but it doesn’t necessarily equate to evidence of rape. In some ways it’s like cultural expression.

It might feel comforting to say, “Well, we can’t fix culture easily, but what we can do is kick people out of group chats or ban fraternities from campus.” But there’s lots of reasons to believe that that’s not going to actually address the root causes. There’s evidence that a lot of young men feel left behind or excluded by prevailing cultural and political and economic forces. I’m not saying that’s factually true, but the feeling seems to have made some young men hateful towards those they perceive to be not like them, who they perceive to be threats to their authority. It’s heartbreaking, not just for the alleged victim of sexual assault, but also for the people who engage in the chat. I don’t think that’s going to give them the sense of security and self that might allow them to behave more generously and respectfully towards others, either. ◼︎

Fraternities control access to coveted resources. They use humiliating rituals to bond with each other, enforce hierarchy, and belittle outsiders. Sound familiar?

What people in Ithaca are saying—and not saying—about the sexual assault allegations brought against members of the Chi Phi fraternity.

#6726. Estée Lauder Companies Sales Climb 6% in Q1

Estée Lauder Companies’s (ELC) 6% sales growth follows a year of continuous growth for the US-based company, while marking an incremental improvement on Q4 2026. The company also updated its forecast for fiscal 2027 to 4-5.5% growth, up from previous guidance of 3-5%.

In line with the company’s ongoing Beauty Reimagined turnaround strategy — initiated in February 2025 with a consumer-first growth objective — ELC reported an adjusted operating margin of 12.1%, up from 7.3%.

“Our strategy is really to accelerate consumer-facing investment,” said CEO Stéphane de La Faverie on Monday. “We are accelerating our top-of-the-funnel investment to build the desirability and awareness of our brands, because this has clearly been one of the key turnarounds for the company.” The shift away from promotional activity, the exec noted, has also been pivotal in maintaining its growth streak, with the company leaning more on building brand equity and desirability.

By category, fragrance led the charge, up an impressive 20% to $861 million — a climb the company attributed to strong performance with Le Labo, Tom Ford, Jo Malone London, and Kilian Paris. Through direct-to-consumer (DTC) channels — free-standing physical stores and online stores — the company tracks its consumer behavior. Notably, in fragrance, consumers are getting younger across the globe, shared de La Faverie. “This young generation is really tailoring their fragrance portfolio based on occasion,” he said. “It can be to go to school, for sports, to go on a date. They [Gen Z] own more fragrances than the generation before.”

Nonetheless, de La Faverie doesn’t want to put all his eggs in one basket. “Overall, our objective is to continue to be broad-based from a growth standpoint,” he said, pointing to upcoming innovations in the skincare category, particularly La Mer and The Ordinary.

Skincare grew 4% to $1.7 billion, bolstered by growth in La Mer and The Ordinary, at opposite ends of the pricing spectrum. Makeup followed, growing 1% to $1 billion, carried by strong performance at Mac Cosmetics, which continues to gain market share in the US’s prestige segment. Haircare was flat at $128 million, carried by The Ordinary but offset by a decline at Aveda, which the company attributed to a supply chain issue (it aims to resolve the problem before the end of Q2).

Regionally, the Asia-Pacific region performed best, up 10%, largely attributed to airport sales. Mainland China followed (9%), pushed by targeted consumer expansion. Europe, UK, Ireland, and emerging markets (EUKEM) was up 4%, matched by the Americas.

Looking ahead, de La Faverie pinpointed the opportunity for market growth in the Asia-Pacific. “We are the leader in fragrances in the Asia-Pacific market, and we are a very strong player in the Chinese market. So you’re seeing a lot of opportunity for additional growth driven by the younger consumer.”

#6723. Young Men of Cornell Want to Talk. Why Are Older Men So Quiet?

Read the rest of GQ’s special report on Cornell here and here.

One night in early October, activists broke a glass door at the entrance of Cornell University’s Day Hall, an administrative building that houses the office of president Michael Kotlikoff. They also vandalized its façade with the messages “Smash the patriarchy” and “At Cornell rapists are protected, protesters are expelled.” The next day, thousands of students and faculty gathered in Cornell’s Ho Plaza. When I stood in front of Day Hall, 36 hours later, much of the evidence had been erased. College life seemed to carry on as normal. Midterms, or “prelims,” were underway; students zoomed stoically past on electric scooters. The shattered panels had been replaced with planks and a sign directing visitors to an alternate entrance, and the graffiti had been power-washed to dark-gray smudges.

Cornell is situated among the gorges of Ithaca, New York, and I drove up from New York City to understand how men were making sense of a civil suit brought in September by an anonymous accuser, called Jane Doe in legal documents and media reports, alleging that she was plied with ketamine and sexually assaulted by seven members of the Chi Phi fraternity in 2024. All the men have denied wrongdoing. Jane Doe’s suit also alleges that Cornell failed to appropriately safeguard her from sexual assault. University administrators acknowledged that they had “fallen short and [are] taking meaningful action.”

According to punishment summaries obtained by The New York Times, Cornell had conducted a Title IX investigation, which resulted in the expulsion of two fraternity brothers and suspension of two others. The Tompkins County District Attorney’s Office declined to pursue criminal charges. (New York governor Kathy Hochul criticized the Cornell police and the Tompkins County DA for their handling of the allegations and appointed attorney general Letitia James as a special prosecutor to investigate.) Among the materials included in Jane Doe’s suit was a photograph of a phone displaying a Chi Phi group chat, with reportedly more than 50 people, in which one of the men advertised Jane Doe’s “free pussy.” Some cheered. One asked if “the shop” was still open. In the screenshot, not a single voice raised an objection.

Men’s perspectives have been underreported during past sexual assault crises, perhaps out of the well-intentioned belief that we should prioritize listening to women. But when such moments to object to a woman’s dehumanization arrive, I wanted to know, why do men so often stay quiet?

Several people told me that my trip coincided with Ithaca’s most beautiful season, and, in person, the landscape did indeed look quintessentially autumnal. (My visit also coincided with the time of year in 2024 that Jane Doe alleges her assault in the Chi Phi house took place.) In the Arts Quad, Oscar, 21, was on his way to class, although he didn’t seem to have anything with him besides his phone. He was dressed in sweats and a boxy yellow tee, and he told me that he had joined a fraternity because he wanted to make friends. He had just left a Sexual Assault Prevention and Safety Club meeting for members of sororities and fraternities, where he and other students had discussed how the Greek system can develop stronger guardrails. One promising approach, he told me, was to delegate prominent members of frats to speak up about sexual assault. “It’s a scary and really hard conversation to have with your friend, especially somebody you think wouldn’t do something like that,” he said. “That’s what our meeting is about: How can we try to make these conversations more normal, more open?” Oscar was generous with his thoughts but grew reserved on the topic of fraternity culture.

Over by the Library or “Libe” Slope, an idyllic stretch of lawn on a rolling hillside, two guys tossed a Frisbee. I sat on the bench next to theirs while they hydrated. “A lot of people are saying, ‘If you were in that group chat, you should have said something,’ which I agree with,” said Hudson Tumey, who graduated last year. “You absolutely should have.” Tumey is a member of Cru, a Christian ministry on campus, and was visiting with William Canlis, a sophomore, who is also involved in Cru. “If I was in that group chat, I would’ve hoped with all my might that I would’ve said something,” Tumey said. “But I think I’d be lying to say that I would’ve for sure said something.” Tumey and Canlis explained that their faith offers them a man to emulate in Jesus Christ. “If a man doesn’t have that standard to look up to, that’s where bad things happen,” Canlis said. “If you don’t have Him, then what role model is left? The world? Look how broken the world is.”

I started looking around for other male role models. Near Sage Chapel, a non-sectarian church on campus, two clergymen visiting the campus said they hadn’t known about the Chi Phi case until that morning. One of them told me he preferred to stay out of it. Just outside Schoellkopf Field, an associate basketball coach asked me to email him. At the ROTC offices in Barton Hall, an Army poster read “Sexual assault. Sexual harassment. Not in our army.” A master sergeant paused his lunch to talk with me. He sat for a moment, then left to call his superior. After five minutes he returned and, with a half-apologetic inhale, said that he couldn’t speak with me until it’s brought up the chain of command and that I should email him. A roving police officer said that the only person on the force approved to speak with the press was Eric Stickel, Cornell University Police Department’s recently appointed chief of police, and I should email him. Ultimately, I approached almost two dozen non-student men associated with the town or the school and only one talked to me on the record.

At the Morrison Dining Hall, a seven-hour sit-in was underway, roughly matching the length of time Jane Doe spent in the Chi Phi house until she lost consciousness, according to the lawsuit. One of the sit-in’s appointed press contacts was John Hartnett, a 19-year-old sophomore representing Students for a Democratic Cornell, a group that had also helped organize the protest earlier in the week. He was proud of that turnout, as it was one of the largest protests at Cornell since the Vietnam War, but incensed by the irony of the school’s history of suspending students involved in other protests. He was also taking inventory of how he and his peers influence each other.

“It’s a difficult thing because you have to realize that some of your past actions—not sexual assault—perpetuated these things,” he said. “It’s something that men have to ask themselves: What am I not doing in terms of not holding the people around me accountable?” My phone rang. Someone from Cornell’s media-relations team informed me that the school was aware of my presence on campus and that none of the administrators were available for comment.

Reporters from Telemundo, The Ithaca Voice, and Reuters descended on Hartnett, and the sheer number of us bombarding him cast the reticence I encountered from his elders in a new light. While an inevitable chorus rose up to defend fraternities or disparage Jane Doe, some young men on campus seemed to be earnestly reckoning with the case or struggling to find their voice. But their community leaders appeared, at most, disconcerted. Perhaps those older men were grappling internally or opting for silent solidarity; whatever their reasons, I was struck by the persistence of their absence.

Later in the evening, I attended a Common Council meeting downtown at Ithaca City Hall. More than 50 people squeezed into a room set up for half that number. Ithaca mayor Robert G. Cantelmo oversaw the proceedings. A dozen or so students found spots on the floor; the local television station was filming. There were complaints regarding the closure of a parking garage, concerns about winter snow plowing, and, eventually, a proposed moratorium on some of Cornell’s event and noise permits, which was put forth as an effort to limit Greek-life activities. The floor was then opened for public comment, but only to those who had filed a slip.

“I think sacrificing some of the fun is more than justified by protecting people from these heinous crimes until the university gets off its rear,” John King, an Ithaca resident, said in support of the moratorium. “I applaud the city government for being willing to do so instead.” Sam Poole was a freshman when Jane Doe first reported her assault to the Cornell police. “I live in a Cornell co-op,” he said. “Co-ops host parties just like fraternities do, plenty of student organizations do, but we decided we’re not going to host parties until the moratorium is over.” Almost a week after the meeting, Cornell banned frat parties for the current academic year.

A few rows behind me, a man in a C-SPAN hoodie and tech pants was growing agitated that he had not been called on. “Michael Tracey,” he called out repeatedly. Mayor Cantelmo explained that Tracey’s name was not among the comment slips given to him by the clerk. “Motion to reconsider,” Tracey yelled out. “You’re definitely not eligible to make motions, because you’re not a member of this body,” Cantelmo replied. Tracey alleged his slip was thrown in the trash. “You should be ashamed for using the power of your office to conclusorily [sic] declare that any American citizen has committed crimes as heinous as gang rape before any semblance of adjudicative process,” he said as he walked out. I passed him my number.

Tracey asked to meet in the lobby of the Canopy Hotel. On my way there I learned that he is a contrarian Substack journalist, known for social media provocation; recently he argued that aspects of the Jeffrey Epstein case were overblown. He’s made multiple trips to Ithaca, where he’s filmed himself cross-examining students and faculty and showing up to public gatherings. He told me that he had driven from Jersey City to Ithaca to report on the Cornell case, motivated by his belief that Jane Doe’s identity should be made public. The previous week, he tweeted out her name before deleting the post. Internet sleuths published her address online. Then, according to Jane Doe’s lawyer, Thomas Giuffra, on the night before the meeting at City Hall, Jane Doe was swatted—that is, someone made a call to the police reporting invented violence at her home, prompting a large-scale police response. Giuffra told CNN that his client has been “terrified.”

Plaintiff anonymity is “used to manipulate the court system and thereby the public as a PR tactic to create pressure,” Tracey told me. “I have nothing against Jane Doe. She seems kind of cool, actually.” He showed me a text he sent to a number he claims belongs to her: “Given that settlement funds are being sought, especially considering that criminal investigations have been triggered, I consider it in the public interest to report on your identity.” When I pointed out that anonymity enables sexual assault victims to come forward with less fear of vitriol and death threats, he described the death threats he has received. This is what one of the loudest male voices I encountered in Ithaca sounds like.

I walked down Ithaca Commons to peer into Moonies, the bar Jane Doe visited before the alleged assault, but it was closed for the night. On Instagram, it appeared to be business as usual, with promotions for “Flash Friday” and “Saturday Surge” parties. In the suit, Jane Doe says she left a sorority event at this bar, went to another bar, and then “stumbled alone” to a party at the Chi Phi house. (Moonies is named in Jane Doe’s suit and its owners have denied that it illegally served alcohol to the underage Jane Doe.) I got into my car and headed to the frat. Even from the cozy confines of my rented Toyota Corolla, that path seemed uninviting. There’s a brick-and-stone charm to downtown Ithaca that dissolves once you cross over the gorges and onto frat row. Flashy trucks and clusters of mega-mansions run by very young adults bordered the drive. I spotted a handful of bongs resting on windowsills. For a minute, I couldn’t find the actual Chi Phi house and when I finally spotted it—massive, menacing—I was nearly relieved.

The next morning, campus cleared out for fall break. Visiting faculty member Mark Brown Jr. sat on the steps of Goldwin Smith Hall and finished a game of virtual chess before speaking with me. “I think frat culture is incredibly toxic,” he said. Some undergraduates in his art classes preferred to discuss the subject in a “roundabout” way. The potential of repercussions, he said, may be deterring open discussion.

I approached two students sitting under a statue of Andrew Dickson White, Cornell’s first president. “All men are honestly guilty for this,” said Daniel Suh, a junior studying public policy. “You ask any man if rape is okay and they’re going to tell you no. But men expect a perfect victim.” His friend, Anand Patel, also a junior, sat next to him. With their baggy pants and gleaming sneakers, Patel and Suh seemed so sweetly boyish, young, and on the brink of adulthood. But also fed up. While Suh felt regulating frat parties wouldn’t directly address the issue of college sexual assault, Patel was much more critical of frat culture. “Where the true issue lies is what happens when you have a friend who does it, or someone you know. Are you going to act like nothing happened? Are you going to support him, or are you going to cut him off? Because that’s ultimately what happens in these frats,” Patel said. “These men don’t draw a line in the sand saying, ‘Okay, you’re out now.’ ”

These students offered a stark contrast with the many older men I had tried to speak to, on and off campus, who couldn’t or wouldn’t add their voices to the conversation. For Patel and the other students I spoke to on campus, things seemed so clear: We should all call out bad behavior when we see it, because we know what bad behavior looks like. When did that get so complicated? ◼︎

Fraternities control access to coveted resources. They use humiliating rituals to bond with each other, enforce hierarchy, and belittle outsiders. Sound familiar?

#6722. Jessica Alba Deletes Instagram Photos After Halloween Costume Backlash

We’ve reached the inevitable part in the Halloween news cycle where celebrities are offending people with their costumes (see: Martha Stewart), and it looks like Jessica Alba’s outfit has incurred some side-eyes.

The multi-hyphenate dropped photos of her costume over the weekend, dressed up as figure skater Nancy Kerrigan with a bloody bandage around her knee. Jessica’s friend, meanwhile, was dressed as Tonya Harding and holding a baseball bat—a clear reference to the 1994 attack on Nancy, which was planned by Tonya’s ex-husband, and led to Tonya being banned.

Jessica has since deleted her Instagram post (the photos are still on Threads), but Us Weekly reports that followers were busy calling her out in the comments, accusing her of being “immature” and writing “Why would you want to revisit this traumatic moment that ended people’s careers?” Meanwhile, commenters on Reddit think the backlash was largely overblown.

For what it’s worth, Jessica wore this costume to Vas J Morgan and Michael Braun’s Halloween Party, which had a “Hockey Horror” theme inspired by Off Campus and Heated Rivalry. Hence, the ice skating outfit!

#6721. The Grooms—and Every Single Guest—Wore White to This Wedding at a Virginia Gilded Age Mansion

While visiting their families for the holidays in Northern Virginia, interior designers Cameron Billinghurst and Nolan Beck Rivera went on a Tinder date. As a sweet gesture, Cameron brought Nolan a single calla lily when they met for coffee. The two lost touch until exactly one year later, on December 26, 2019, when they bumped into each other at the National Museum of African American History and Culture. “We reconnected and started talking every day,” Cameron says. During COVID, the pair became even closer and decided to move in together in Richmond, Virginia. “We figured we could just rent a place and leave if we didn’t like it, but we found such an amazing creative community here,” says Cameron. “We’re six years in, and I don’t see us moving.” Not only did they become romantic partners, but they also co-founded a design firm, Studio Tarea, which focuses on bespoke interiors for residential and commercial spaces.

While it wasn’t a proposal, Nolan did have rings made for them both with The Circle Craft just a few months into dating. “I avoided calling them engagement rings for a while, and ‘promise ring’ had a weird ring to it,” he says. “So I just kind of said, ‘Oh here, I made us matching rings,’ and that was it.” In fact, there was no proposal at all when the couple started planning their wedding. “We started talking about what we’d both want out of a wedding and after a few years of that, we decided to just put a date on the calendar,” says Cameron. Adds Nolan, “Not very romantic, but we’re pragmatists. It just kind of felt like it was going to happen from the beginning.”

As they kicked off planning, the hospitality-inclined pair wanted to put guest experience first throughout the day and tap their creative community to bring everything to life. “From the get-go, we knew who we could trust to take our ideas and build something cool on top of them,” says Cameron. “We’ve also been to many weddings and made sure to include the things we loved while skipping some of the expected traditions that didn’t resonate.” Jokes Nolan, “We really streamlined a lot of the romance out of this thing. We just wanted everyone to arrive, have enough to eat and drink, and cut a rug with us.” He adds, “There were no jerks at this wedding. Everyone that was working it and everyone we invited was just the best.”

The to-be-weds set their date for June 6, 2026, at the Branch Museum of Design in Richmond. “It’s an old Gilded Age mansion that is now a design museum in Richmond’s Fan neighborhood,” explains Nolan. As the couple hoped to infuse their cultural backgrounds throughout the affair, this venue decision helped tie in a part of their history. “While the food, music, and much of the entertainment were informed by our respective mothers’ Honduran and African-American cultures, we felt our English-descended dads reflected in the Tudor Revival mansion where the whole thing took place,” says Cameron.

James Beard Award semi-finalist chef Brittany Anderson was a key player in bringing together this ethos of hospitality and culture. “We wanted the experience to be food-centric,” says Nolan. “She was able to weave together Honduran and Black Southern influences into a very unique menu that was very specific to us.” Floral design was also vital to the entire aesthetic of the celebration thanks to the work of Meredith Wheeler of Secret Flowers. “We wanted everything to be minimal and modern in contrast to the Tudor Revival mansion we were in,” explains Cameron. “We’re modernists, and I think the florals really helped keep the wedding in a modernist space.” The final team member who helped execute the celebration was Bryce Carson of Carson Ashby Events, who served as day-of coordinator and helped with last logistical details of the event. “I know that the wedding wouldn’t have gone nearly as smoothly without Bryce,” adds Nolan.

The dress code for the weekend was set with every groom and guest in the same palette. “We decided a long time ago that we wanted to do a dress code of white to beige for all guests,” says Cameron. “We were as excited or more excited to see what friends and family would be wearing than we were about our own looks.” Many friends wore vintage, some designed their own looks, and others even wore their own wedding dresses. “Including my ballet teacher, who wore her original wedding dress from long enough ago that it would be considered vintage today,” adds Nolan.

That said, the couple did put care into their attire. Cameron donned a wool silk suit by Grand Le Mar and black Jacques Solovière loafers. To accessorize, he wore a silver bracelet that belonged to his grandma Eddie Mae and a vintage Seiko tank watch. Nolan’s outfit went through a few trials. “I’d ordered a custom outfit with a matador-style jacket and big, cool design-y pants from a designer on the internet who turned out to be a scammer,” he says. “So I ended up having to go to SuitSupply in Washington, DC only a few weeks before the wedding, and it arrived just before the big day.”

However, the outfit wasn’t perfect just yet. “They had made a mistake with the pants, so we took them to Lida at Roya Alterations here in Richmond—another act of Bryce heroism—and she fixed them the day before the wedding,” Nolan says. He accented the look with silver and pearl calla lily earrings lent by a friend, vintage cufflinks in similar materials, his father’s watch, and Saint Laurent boots. “I bought them when I got one of my first big checks as a self-employed person and walked the heels off of them, so I had them redone before the wedding,” he adds.

Weekend festivities began with a picnic in the park in the gardens of the Virginia Museum of Fine Arts. “More than 80 of our 128 guests were from out of town, and it gave us all an opportunity to spend a little more time together,” says Nolan. That evening, the grooms had an intimate rehearsal dinner at the home of their friends Lucia Zolea and Patrick Inskeep, who are also parents to their flower girl, Jane. “We’d designed their garden a couple of years ago, and it was a really special place to share with our loved ones and have dinner,” says Cameron. Dinner came from the couple’s favorite, Ronnie’s Original BBQ. “Ronnie himself was there, which is kind of a big deal,” adds Nolan.

As the guests arrived on the wedding day, they were treated to cocktails and delicious bites before taking a seat in the ceremony area. On the lawn, the floral team crafted a chapel in-the-round of corn stalks, grasses, and Spanish moss with guests seated in four quadrants around the center. “We had a gospel choir off to one side singing a cappella,” explains Cameron. “My grandmother used to take me to church with her, and this was our way of bringing her into the ceremony. Guests were given white paddle fans, and the result felt romantic and rooted in the South in a non-traditional kind of way.”

The grooms walked down the aisle to “Never Let Me Go” by Florence and the Machine sung by the choir and had the ceremony officiated by their close friend Lanecia Rouse. “Asking Lanecia to officiate was actually one of the first decisions we made for the wedding,” says Cameron. “Lanecia also gave a brief history of jumping the broom and its significance in the Black community, and reminded everyone (in a fun, beautiful way) that the rights that we ourselves were exercising that day (interracial marriage and gay marriage) were both hard-fought and not necessarily secure for future generations, which makes it especially beautiful that we’re able to do this and that everyone showed up for us.” The grooms reflect how the ceremony really made them feel as if they were part of a community.

Next came dinner inside the mansion. “We wanted to keep table arrangements very pared back to accommodate family-style service and guest interaction,” says Cameron. “The final result—stalks of corn on simple chrome bases—was pretty striking when you entered the space, but also incredibly practical at dinner.” Guests were seated based on thoughtful decisions by the grooms. “One of the most fun parts for us of the planning process was creating the seating chart for dinner and imagining all the ways we could connect our loved ones from all over,” explains Cameron. “It felt really special to see it happen in real time. We only invited lovely people that we wanted there, and everyone gelled so perfectly.” Chef Anderson served a menu of grilled chicken, mushroom risotto, veggies, and ticucos, a tamale-like dish native to Honduras. Cheerwine cake from Chef Leah Branch sweetened up the night further.

The grooms broke into a custom disco ball piñata to start the reception. “We filled it with lollipops, chocolate, flavored condoms, poppers, wigs, fake mustaches, candy cigarettes, heart-shaped glasses, and a silver thong,” says Nolan. “I don’t know who got the thong.” The dance party started as a hundred mylar balloons floated down from the choir loft and a kaleidoscopic video of flowers was projected onto the dance floor. Adds Nolan, “Every minute or two a balloon would explode, and everyone would cheer and just keep dancing.” In addition to dancing and mingling, guests could have a tattoo done by Vincent Riquier of The Ceremony Tattoo. “The most popular designs were a drawing of a calla lily that I’ve had tattooed on my shoulder since Cameron and I got together, and the word Amor in my handwriting,” says Nolan.

The grooms did a grand sparkler exit and sped off on Nolan’s 1973 BMW R75/5 with cans tied to the back. Afterward, many guests headed off to Babe’s of Carytown, the oldest lesbian bar on the East Coast. “We hung out there until about 2 a.m., dancing and catching up with all of the people who came into town to celebrate us,” says Cameron. “We closed the night across the street at 7-11 with celebratory Slurpees.” Adds Nolan, “Drinking a bright red Slurpee in a white tux is pretty harrowing, but we just really wanted Slurpees.”

Looking back, the grooms share how wonderful it was to have their community contribute to their celebration and to have so many details feel specific to their story. “It’s the best wedding I’ve ever been to,” says Nolan.

We designed the invitations ourselves and used photos by our friends Lucia Zolea and Kate Jennings. The vintage calla lily brooch was a gift from Lucia, too, and the back of the rehearsal dinner invitation was a photo of her garden, which we designed and is where we had our rehearsal dinner.

Cameron wore a wool silk suit and Nolan wore a tuxedo. Our goal was to look sharp and well-dressed, but we really wanted to create the space for all of our guests’ outfits to shine through as well.

We got married in a 1919 Tudor Revival mansion in Richmond that now houses the Branch Museum of Design.

I borrowed my dad’s vintage Seiko watch, and the bracelet was from a friend’s shop, Na Nin. —Nolan

We decided to get ready together in what was Mr. Branch’s wood-paneled bedroom. Mrs. Branch’s bedroom is where family and close friends got dressed.

It was so special to have so many friends from all over the world in one place.

We loved that, even with the somewhat strict dress code, there were no outfit repeats and everyone was able to bring their own perspective and voice.

My sister’s shoes by Cult Gaia. She wore a dress that was inspired by Greek statuary. Her rings were made by our friends at The Circle Craft. I helped design the rose a few years ago. —Nolan

This patio on the roof was actually a great spot to micromanage everything from.

The couple who built the house had a really robust collection of Renaissance art. We loved the look of modernity in front of a 16th-century Flemish tapestry.

Nolan made our gold bands with The Circle Craft shortly after we started dating. Our wedding rings are from the Mayan city of Copán in Honduras, which we’ve visited a few times. They’re silver with pieces of different semi-precious stones inlaid. —Cameron

We opted for an in-the-round ceremony so we could be surrounded by the people we love.

Meredith at Secret Flowers created this incredible room out of tall grasses and moss.

Cameron brought me a single calla lily on our first date six years ago and they’ve held their importance to us since. —Nolan

Guests were greeted with chilled peanut soup with sunflower chili crisp as they arrived.

Our stylish friend Sam in a J.Crew suit and a shirt by local brand Ledbury. He made the tie himself.

Audra wore a tuxedo and oversized sculptural earrings. She and her seven-year-old daughter came up with the idea of using one of them as a corsage.

Several people made their own outfits. Some people wore their wedding dresses. And the only person who didn’t get the memo blended right in once he took his jacket off.

Our flower girl, Jane, with her mother, Lucia, and our ring bearer, Robby, with his father, Bobby. We’ve been so lucky to be a part of Jane and Robby’s lives since they were born and it was really amazing to have them there on the day.

Jane did an excellent job as the flower girl. She wore the sweetest little dress from Tia Cibani.

Our good friend Lanecia officiated the ceremony.

We walked down the aisle to a gospel choir singing “Never Let Me Go” by Florence + the Machine.

Entering the circle. It was a singular experience.

Lanecia approached the ceremony so thoughtfully and spoke about love, history, and the cultural context of the ceremony.

I grew up going to a Southern Baptist church with my late grandmother, Eddie, and the music was always a really special part of the experience. I knew I wanted that cultural connection to be a part of the day from early on. Four vocalists from Gospel Touch sang selections for guests before and during the ceremony. —Cameron

We had a 12-minute ceremony, then it was off to dinner.

My other love, my 1973 BMW R75/5. I made the heart and tied ribbons around soup cans earlier in the week. —Nolan

My friend Arlene thrifted that shawl about an hour before the wedding. —Nolan

Our cocktails were mignonette martinis and mojitos. The mignonette is a signature drink at Bar Buoy, a restaurant we designed. And we drink a lot of mojitos.

Judson and Quinton having a chat after the ceremony. We made sure everyone had drinks and food from the moment they arrived to the moment they left.

I actually think the seating chart was like a fun puzzle where we got to introduce people we thought would get along. Watching people come together ended up being one of the best parts of the wedding. —Cameron

We asked Meredith to go super minimal with the table decor. We wanted something tall and dramatic but that wouldn’t interfere with cross-table conversations.

All in all, pretty minimalist tables. Each place setting had a scented candle with a special scent that we developed with Na Nin. The place cards were hand-etched by Leah Schmidt.

We have 800 of these matchbooks leftover if you need any.

Dinner was served family style. We had grilled chicken, mushroom risotto, cornbread, and ticucos, a pre-Hispanic sort of tamale from the mountains of Honduras. Everything aside from the chicken was vegan. Chef Brittanny Anderson knocked it out of the park.

Chef Leah Branch came to personally plate and serve the cake. Instead of doing a big central cake, we chose to have individual portions of her Cheerwine cake that she serves at The Roosevelt, one of our favorite restaurants where we signed our marriage certificate last year.

We had a local Latin candy shop, Dulceria Minilandia, make our disco-ball piñata. We filled it with candy cigarettes, wigs, condoms, poppers, fake mustaches, and chocolate... and one silver thong.

Friends, neighbors, and family watching us bash a piñata.

90 out of 100 of these balloons exploded.

Our neighbors, Michael and Arlinda, looking sharp in their mustaches.

Murilo getting inked. Some of our guests got their first-ever tattoos that night.

Friend and clothing designer Maya Shaw looking incredible.

Jordan and Kael. Jordan wore the first wedding dress she bought for their wedding last year but ended up not wearing. Kael is wearing the shirt that Jordan made for him to wear at their wedding.

We had a projection of a floral kaleidoscope across the dance floor. It looked really stunning and distorted across everyone’s white outfits.

Saoli, Emi, Alisha, and Jacqueline enjoying their skinny European cigarettes. Alisha actually made her own dress for the night.

Driving off on the BMW. We just parked around the corner then got in a friend’s car to hit the gay club. —Nolan

#6718. Adria Arjona Has Come Full Circle

Adria Arjona doesn’t like glam that feels like a transformation. A self-described tomboy growing up, Arjona says she avoided makeup at all costs; but through her journey to becoming a Hollywood actor, she’s been fortunate to work with enough pros who’ve helped her define what she likes. “I’ve discovered that with makeup you can enhance yourself without changing the way that you look, and that makes me feel really empowered, beautiful, and sexy because it’s still me,” she tells Vogue. “Now I believe less is more.”

Some might find it ironic, then, that a makeup minimalist would go on to become a face of one of the world’s most known cosmetics brands; Arjona was just announced as the newest L’Oréal Paris global ambassador. For the actor and producer, this partnership hits far closer to home than makeup itself.

Half Puerto Rican, half Guatemalan, Arjona tells me she grew up all over Latin America, where she didn’t have consistent access to TV shows and movies. “Commercials were my most consistent thing,” she says with a laugh. “The L’Oréal Paris commercial is so ingrained in my head. I just remember playing it with my little friends and being like ‘Porque tú lo vales.’ I've been doing that for so long, so to be able to now be an ambassador and be able to say ‘I’m worth it’ to a camera, it’s surreal.”

Arjona was in the middle of filming The Thomas Crown Affair, the romantic heist film set to debut in March 2027, when she got the news. She remembers telling seven of her closest female friends and family members, who happened to be relaxing in her living room with her: “We let out this huge scream.” Fast-forward to Arjona’s campaign shoot, and the excitement hadn’t turned down a notch. “My mom was there [with me], and we both looked at each other like, Oh my God, this is the coolest thing in the world.”

Filming felt like a big, glamorous movie set, she continues, though everyone was incredibly warm and welcoming. I ask her what she hoped to bring to the campaign, and she admits it’s a tricky question. “You’re coming into a company that has empowered women for so long that yes, you want to bring your essence, but I’m Latin American—I want to bring my own sort of spice.” Now, she’s focusing on learning from the other ambassadors and the company itself. She nods specifically to L’Oréal Paris’s ongoing philanthropic work, envisioning how the two can unite to continue uplifting women—a cause the actor champions through her own foundation.

The campaign, directed by Colin Cardo, takes us to the Théâtre du Châtelet in Paris, where Arjona introduces us to the new Volume Liberté mascara. Created for more than 800% more volume in a single application, the formula delivers soft, clump-free lift. The smudge-free, flake-resistant formula is housed inside a sleek click-to-release tube that adds a touch of luxury to your average mascara—so much so that Arjona was shocked when she first tried it. “It almost feels like a piece of jewelry in your bag, or something super high-end and elevated,” she says. “I’m a very big mascara girl. This makes your lashes so beautiful and bold.”

The shape of the brush, she continues, is what takes this formula to the next level. “During the day, I don’t like to look like I have mascara on, but I want my lashes to look a little bit darker and longer,” she says. It’s one product she doesn’t leave the house without.

True to her no-makeup makeup ethos, her daily look is about enhancing her features. Because most days start with working out, she’ll shower and let her curls air-dry with the L’Oréal Paris Elvive mousse: “It’s not crunchy, and it looks really natural.” When it comes to makeup, she’s obsessed with multipurpose products. Along with Volume Liberté, she keeps the shade Worth It Intense of the Blurfiller Lip Liner in her purse; she reaches for it every day to define her lips, yes, but also to contour when she wants to take her daytime look to the next level: “I know it’s not designed to do that, but I’ll just go for it.” For glow, she swears by the Lumi Glotion for face and body radiance, and the Colour Riche Satin Lipstick for special occasions.

“I like makeup to to kind of enhance your own beauty, but not change too much,” she says. “I don’t like looking like I have a lot of makeup on.” As a lifelong L’Oréal Paris fan, this moment means a lot to her. “It’s just so powerful; [being] an ambassador feels really full-circle to me. I definitely don’t take it lightly.”

#6716. The Art of the Evening Sweater: 5 Silhouettes to Wear All Winter Long

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These sumptuous knits are anything but frumpy.

While I love nothing more than burrowing into my most oversized, shapeless cashmere pullover during the colder months, I admit that it doesn’t feel like the most appropriate sartorial choice for a night out. When I want to feel sexy without spending the whole night shivering, I reach for the unsung hero of my winter wardrobe: the evening sweater.

This season, we’ve seen sexy knitwear in the form of cutout cable knits at Schiaparelli and see-through cardigans at Tom Ford—and the trend translates seamlessly off the runway, too. For the most wearable option, look for a knit with a single element that leans more sultry: a draped neckline that falls off the shoulder, a wrap silhouette that hugs the waist, or a deep plunge that shows off some décolletage. Ahead, see the evening sweater trends to shop for the colder days ahead.

With its ballerina-inspired silhouette, a wrap sweater offers timeless appeal. Alaïa’s and & Other Stories’s takes on the style offer an adjustable wrap design, while Naked Cashmere’s rendition adds a gold-toned pin closure for an elevated touch.

There’s something undeniably sensual about a top that exposes your collarbone, especially in the form of plush cashmere and merino wool knitwear. Whether you prefer Staud’s slouchy one-shoulder pick or Simkhai’s halter straps, these styles have a come-hither effect without trying too hard.

A daring neckline is a surefire way to heat up your cold-weather ensembles, and we saw down-to-there sweaters dominate the runways at Zimmermann and Prada. From Reformation’s chunky pullover to Same’s barrel silhouette, these knits pair nicely with your laciest bra.

Backless sweaters are an unexpected pick for date nights, especially when contrasted with a higher neckline. Standout picks from Lisa Yang, Aritzia, and Tove appear deceptively demure from the front for maximum impact.

For a less bundled-up option, reach for a sleeveless sweater. A halterneck silhouette offers an unexpected take on fall’s most ubiquitous layer while still fitting comfortably beneath a leather jacket, and Ossou’s slinky Halo Top is a perfect match for low-rise denim and a faux fur coat.
