#6714. Are Frats Just Gangs by Another Name?

This article contains descriptions of allegations of sexual assault. Read the rest of GQ’s special report on Cornell here and here.

I grew up in Amherst, Massachusetts, 10 minutes from frat row, a strip of filthy, dilapidated mansions long since demolished and bought by UMass. When I was a child, I went trick-or-treating there and received rolls of toilet paper for my trouble. Then, as a teenager, I went there because it seemed like a place where nobody would stop me from drinking, or otherwise try to protect me from anything. It was a kind of slumming, an escape to a decrepit environment mostly beyond the reach of the law. The first time I hung out with people who drank themselves sick, it was at a frat party. My first rock show and mosh pit too. Driving around town, my friends and I chauffeured hitchhiking frat boys from one party to another. The town was full of people who liked to criticize rich people, but frat boys and other students actually threw stuff at rich people. As a longstanding winter tradition, UMass students marched onto the campus of small, elite Amherst College, pelting students with snowballs, but also, reportedly one year with rocks, obliging Amherst College’s president, the following year, to threaten to call in the state police. But to me, their apparent lawlessness made them exciting, the local equivalent of Vikings.

Later, I wrote a book of short stories about frat boys, interviewing many of them in the course of my research. So when news spread that a woman had filed a civil lawsuit alleging a horrifying gang rape had taken place at the Cornell chapter of the Chi Phi fraternity in 2024, people asked me how such a thing might have happened.

Much of the Cornell affair is shocking. But it’s especially repugnant that the young men in question, who have denied wrongdoing, apparently wanted to have sex with a woman who was, according to the plaintiff’s account, so far gone on alcohol and ketamine that she could barely be considered conscious. “I was a sex doll for all intents and purposes,” Jane Doe told a police interviewer, according to reporting by The New York Times. Her intoxication, according to the lawsuit, was extreme. Indeed, it could have been a kind of coping strategy: “My thought was, if I do ketamine again, maybe I will pass out,” she’s reported to have told officials. And what was happening inside the minds of the men? The suit includes a photograph of a Snapchat group, where one of the brothers texted other active members to “pu” (“pull up”) for “free pussy.” What could make a man advertise an intoxicated woman’s body for the apparent purposes of sharing her with his male friends?

Since Jane Doe’s civil lawsuit was filed, much has been written about hazing rituals in general, which some pundits argue socialize members to be violent toward pledges, and women. But there is also a blend of homophobia and homoeroticism that has a long history in American fraternities. The anthropologist Peggy Reeves Sanday, in her 1990 book Fraternity Gang Rape, related the account from a woman who lived in a frat house for a summer and described a “circle dance” that took place, invariably, to the Doors’ song “L.A. Woman”:

The song comes on and the brothers start yelling “Ho! Ho! Ho!” All the brothers link arms and join in a circle. Then the whole circle moves around so fast that the shortest members are lifted off the ground. The “Ho!”s continue until the circle loses control and the entire group piles on top of each other, squashing the smallest members […] For a bunch of guys who were always so full of nasty, ugly, homophobic remarks, they seemed to enjoy jumping around on top of each other very much.

In some cases, there have been homosexuality-detection rituals in fraternities that are not only implicitly anti-gay but deeply homoerotic. Pledges, according to one of the accounts in Sanday’s book, were told to expose their genitals, with actives (an “active” is an active member, that is, a full-fledged brother of the fraternity) punishing those who refused with push-ups. Other rituals are just plain homoerotic: Pledges might be asked to simulate sexual acts in front of others, or forced to shower together, or consume liquor through the anus.

There’s a history, too, of frat boys using women as accessories for homoerotic revelry. In 2015, the Indiana University chapter of the Alpha Tau Omega fraternity had its charter revoked after a leaked video showed a crowd of actives watching as one of their brothers, prone on a mattress and stripped to his boxers and socks, looks to be performing cunnilingus on a woman, who, the chapter’s chief executive officer said, was a hired exotic dancer, and whose legs seem to keep him in a headlock. Some men sit on the floor nearly naked, watching passively, while others stand over him fully clothed, spanking their brother or patting his back. It’s unclear to what degree, if any, the frat boy giving oral sex is being coerced, but it seems clear from his state of undress that he’s been cast as an object of desire. The woman appears to restrain him, forcing his face into her crotch. She is there, it seems, to perform dominance for both the amusement and the titillation of the frat.

The horrors alleged at Cornell seem to have taken place in that space between amusement and titillation. One of the Chi Phi’s on the now infamous Snapchat included in the lawsuit about the alleged gang rape sent a text about how a brother “went in 7 min ago and is not afk” (“away from keyboard”). Another, one of the men in the purported original threesome with Jane Doe, added something seemingly like an exhortation: “Like you can walk in and whip it out.” If word had gotten around Chi Phi that a threesome had taken place between two of the brothers and Jane Doe at the proposition of one of the men, as is alleged to have happened, they might well have been ridiculed or, worse, made to feel like outsiders. Their brothers might have asked, Who did what with whom? Did they like it? Some of their brothers, in other words, might have made a supposition about their sexuality.

It’s possible to interpret the Snapchat messages as an effort to turn a MMF threesome—with its potential suggestion of male bisexuality—into something more heterosexual. According to Jane Doe’s lawsuit, one of the two Chi Phi’s “forced oral sex on Plaintiff” while the other one “slapped Plaintiff on the buttocks multiple times, resulting in bruising.” According to the claims in the lawsuit, this might have been the beginning of a long slide into the alleged drug-fueled violence—Jane Doe treated as a shared resource, like a pizza or a keg.

According to Thomas Giuffra, Jane Doe’s lawyer, she learned of the Snapchat exchange thanks to a picture taken by a fraternity member not present. A few days after the alleged assault, she saw other users condemning Chi Phi on an online forum, as detailed in text messages accessed by The New York Times.

“Are Chi Phi brothers Crips or Bloods because I hear they have been doing hell Gang Banging?”

“They do drugs and gang r*ped a sophomore. Report them.”

Those posts reportedly started to shift Jane Doe’s perspective about what had happened to her. “That’s the first time I had ever heard the narrative change that I wasn’t a whore and that I was assaulted,” she reportedly told investigators. The posts laid bare a chilling facet of Greek life: A fraternity that discards its proclaimed values (“Chivalrous behavior crosses the ages to touch the heart of Chi Phi,” reads the creed of the frat) can start to look like a middle-class gang.

In a foreword to Fraternity Gang Rape, longtime Philadelphia trial judge Lois G. Forer remarked on the similarities between frat boys and urban street gang members, having tried both in her courtroom. Fraternities and gangs, she said, both “engage in sexual behavior that others call gang rape,” considering it “a form of male bonding for which the female is merely an available instrument.”

But there is another similarity that Forer neglected to mention: a fraternity, like a street gang, can derive much of its power by controlling access to an illicit resource. A street gang might control access to cocaine or heroin in a particular neighborhood; a fraternity, at a university located in a small city like Ithaca or Amherst, can act as the gatekeepers to a large, conveniently located house in which there might be little to no supervision or check on the consumption of drugs and alcohol. That is what brought me, as a curious teenager, to frat row, where underage drinking and other mischief appeared to be abundant.

According to text messages published by CBS, about a week after allegedly being raped at the Chi Phi house, and a couple of days after seeing the “Crips or Bloods” comments online, Jane Doe seemed to consider attending Chi Phi’s upcoming Halloween party.

“Chi phi is your house and you are welcome here,” a frat brother wrote, reassuringly. “Thank u i really appreciate that,” she replied. “<3 i’ll bring my cigs tomorrow.”

Soon after, though, she reportedly said she received a call from the chapter president disinviting her from the party. The dismay and anger she expressed in response—“yall aren’t my friends” and “the woman will always get the short end of the stick”—seem to be a testament to how central Chi Phi’s parties were to her social world, to Chi Phi’s outsize power to ostracize in the insular eddy of the American college campus.

Those within a fraternity fear ostracization, too, and they might take dramatic measures to avoid it. In Sanday’s book, she describes a brother “so worried about his attractiveness and masculinity that he set quotas for sexual encounters for certain time periods.” Sex was not merely about pleasure, “but the feeling of acceptance and approval” it might generate. The alleged violence at Cornell smacks of these anxieties, the need to transform sexual intercourse into a performance of masculinity. A healthy fraternity, committed to the cultivation of young men, might allay these anxieties rather than exacerbate them. The frat would be a place to practice alternative forms of masculinity, inculcated by a house where vice had no foothold. But then, would anyone go to their parties? Would the fraternity even survive?

As a high school kid, I wanted to live within a fraternity’s nimbus of approval. Instead, I lived with my mother, most of the time, and it was she who had taught me how to shave. I didn’t play any sports, wasn’t on a team or in a club. I was drawn to fraternities, then, for some of the same reasons boys in poor neighborhoods are drawn to gangs: loneliness, boredom, the need for male role models.

One autumn night, not long after rush week, I walked by one of the frat houses and witnessed a spectacle unfolding on a lawn: pledges, arrayed single-file, dressed for a day at the office, in sweater vests, ties, oxford shirts, khakis, and brogues. An active, pacing the line like a drill sergeant assessing the combat readiness of new recruits, barked about the importance of neat tie-knots and aftershave, of steaming your pants and polishing your shoes. The pledges listened in silence, rapt with attention, their ties crooked, their sweater vests ill-fitting, their bearing less than military, some of them fidgeting, and shifting their weight from foot to foot. They would have done anything their pledgemaster said, so total was his air of authority and their need for it. I would have too—believing, in my innocence, that he could have taught me how to be a man. ◼︎

A fraternity at UC Berkeley in the 2000s.

In 2018’s The American Fraternity, artist Andrew Moisey presented intimate photographs of Greek life at his alma mater (see above) to reveal the paradox of fraternities: Pledges take oaths to help each other become ideal men, then revel in behaving badly. Now a professor of art history and visual studies at Cornell, Moisey tells GQ: “It feels [like] we are in an abusive relationship with the fraternities. They tell the task forces that they’ll never do it again, but they’re built on breaking their own promises. If the fraternity is helping Cornell’s graduates go on to high places, then, I guess, isn’t that good for Cornell? But when there’s a death [like that of Antonio Tsialas, whose body was found at the bottom of a gorge two days after an unauthorized Cornell fraternity party in 2019] or an alleged rape or any other kind of tragedy, our association means that what they do dishonors all of us.” For Moisey, the debate now playing out among faculty, administrators, trustees, and donors about the future of frats at Cornell boils down to an “old, classic” question: “What’s more important, honor or money? Cornell is supposed to represent enlightenment. Does this look like enlightenment to you?”

What people in Ithaca are saying—and not saying—about the sexual assault allegations brought against members of the Chi Phi fraternity.

A sociologist who studies online groups explains how social dynamics play out on our screens.

#6713. Daniel Roseberry Ran the New York City Marathon … And Only Started Training Two Months Ago

Marathon Sunday was a blur, but Schiaparelli creative director Daniel Roseberry does remember a few things vividly: the dappled light across the 26.2-mile route; the rhythmic sounds of sneakers against pavement as he strode onto a bridge and the crowds faded away; the absolutely perfect weather; and the overwhelming feeling of love he felt as he saw his friends and family on the sidelines, cheering him on.

“There are no words to describe it,” Roseberry tells Vogue of running the New York City Marathon, his first. “This day will stay with me … for a long time.”

The funny thing is, this time last year Roseberry had no intention of taking on the arduous course. But last Christmas, the designer went out on an impulsive—and what would prove to be fateful—run through Paris along the Seine, the city’s famous central waterway. While he was no stranger to the magic and romance of the city where he has lived and worked since 2019, the run opened his eyes to his surroundings in new and profound ways. Something awoke in him, and, ten months later, he found himself among the nearly 60,000 participants of the New York City Marathon, which he completed in 4 hours and 24 minutes.

“It was just the perfect day,” Roseberry says a few hours after finishing, his medal still draped around his shoulders. He’d just enjoyed some post-race pizza and pasta (a “second dinner” of Shake Shack was also on its way), and he was looking ahead to some much-deserved time in Therabody recovery boots. “It really reconnected me to my love of this city.”

Roseberry started the day early, at 3:30 a.m., before taking a 5:00 a.m. bus to Staten Island, where the race begins before winding its way through the remaining four boroughs and ending in Central Park. “It was pin-drop silent on the bus,” he recalls. “But you can sense everyone’s energy. It’s like this pent-up anticipation.” He was part of the last wave, which set off at 11:30 a.m., meaning that after he arrived he had to wait five hours before starting his journey.

After scaling the route’s opening gambit—the four-mile-long Verrazzano-Narrows Bridge, with a two-mile, 174-foot elevation gain, the race’s highest climb—the infamous crowds that line the route started to materialize. “The city really shows up, and it’s beautiful. All these people cheering for you… and they don’t know you. They’re cheering your name, telling you you can do it. It really carries you and is a beautiful representation of humanity at its best. It transcended every expectation I had.”

Roseberry completed this feat with just two months of training, starting in September, as he was preparing his spring 2027 ready-to-wear collection. He worked with Jonathan Fins, his trainer of two years, to structure a program that built on his existing fitness foundation, rooted in strength training, mobility, and calisthenics. (Roseberry can not only design a shockingly glamorous dress but also do a deeply impressive 24 pull-ups.) It was truncated and intense: In September, the pair added a weekly run that increased in mileage, peaking at 33 kilometers (around 20 miles) before tapering, so as not to exhaust the body in the lead-up to the main event. (It’s common practice for first-time marathoners not to run the full 26.2 until the actual race.)

The training process allowed him a break from his usual responsibilities, in which he’s the one calling the shots. “Most days, everyone’s looking to me for answers,” he says. “With this, I was able to put myself into the care of another person.”

Roseberry had no specific finish time in mind. “I just want to finish, and be able to walk,” he half-joked during a FaceTime call the day before the race. He did later note, however, that he’d acheived one objective: he went through the entire course without walking.

The designer chose New York as the location for his first race because he was already scheduled to be in town to accept the Leader in the Arts Award at the Hirshhorn Gala award on Monday. With Halloween rolling into Marathon Day, however, he also realized that it was one of the best weekends to be in the city. “It just had to be New York,” he says.

During training, Roseberry would spend the first half of his run talking (and sometimes bickering) with Fins, only to reward himself with a pop-heavy playlist for the second half (Olivia Rodrigo, Taylor Swift, and Rosalía were in heavy rotation). And while he cued up an epic song cycle for the actual marathon, he ended up only listening to it for 15 minutes. “It didn’t feel like it was in the spirit of the day,” he says. “I wanted to be in the moment, honor it, and take it all in. I wanted to be present with the other runners, and with the people who came to watch.”

Roseberry’s $15 sunglasses, found at Dick’s Sporting Goods in Maine.

As one would imagine, Roseberry was very particular about his training attire: Asics Gel-Kayano running shoes, Nike socks, open-weave cotton tank tops from Sunspel, and cheap, sporty wraparound sunglasses that he bought in multiple colors from Dick’s Sporting Goods in Maine over the summer.

And training for and then completing the marathon gave him great insight into his own work as a designer. “It had me thinking about the body in different ways,” he says. “How could it not? And I like that the marathon is this thing with a defined beginning and end; it’s a closed loop. There are so many things always going on in fashion, but I’d like to take that mentality into my work.”

Post-marathon glow. The T-shirt features an image of Roseberry’s head superimposed onto Julie Andrews’s in The Sound of Music, an inside joke between him and his friends.

Roseberry poses with his friends and family after finishing.

On the morning of the race, Roseberry sent a voice note, reflecting on what the two-month, intensive program had meant to him. “The thing I keep coming back to is this physical transformation is so important to me. It feels so vital because it’s a physical manifestation of an inner transformation that’s been happening over the past year,” he said. “I think for four decades I had been completely, in many ways, living for the approval of other people and living in total abandonment of myself, and my body, for sure. I think this marathon has significance because it’s the first real thing to be envisioned, worked and trained for, and completed in a really radical, new way of thinking. The transformation isn’t transforming into somebody different or somebody new or something better. It’s about remembering what my body was always capable of in the first place. I feel like I met this body, like it’s a person, that I re-met a year ago. And the last eight weeks of training have been a way of honoring that relationship going back into my life.”

At one point during our call after the race, Roseberry mentions something about next year, which begged the question: will he be back? “Oh, yeah,” he says. “Early on in the race, like mile four or five, I called my friend and told her, ‘We’re so doing this next year.’”

#6711. Keira Knightley’s Theater Look Is a Regal Erdem Runway Fit

She celebrated The Lives of Others opening with husband James Righton

On Thursday evening, Keira Knightley and the cast of The Lives of Others celebrated the official opening night of their West End production, joined by family, friends, and colleagues at London’s Adelphi Theater for a special performance. And afterwards, they took things to Brasserie Zedel for a sparkling after party.

Following her final bow, Knightley got ready for the night ahead by changing into a striking two-piece set from Erdem’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection: a cropped V-neck sweater and long ballgown skirt. The knitwear, a dark-gray piece that put an elevated, regal twist on a cozy fall basic, was designed with long sleeves, a fitted silhouette, and a sculptural scalloped hem that was trimmed with crystal appliqués.

The beading effortlessly merged into the embroidery on the semi-sheer tulle skirt, creating a fluid ensemble that, at first glance, could easily read as a singular dress.

The Oscar-winning actor wore the runway fit with Roger Vivier’s “I Love Polka Dot” slingbacks—black mesh pumps with a heart-shaped edge and velvet polka dot detailing. She was joined by her husband, James Righton, who wore a classic navy pinstripe suit over a blue dress shirt.

Adapted for stage from Florian Henckel von Donnersmarck’s Oscar-winning 2006 film, The Lives of Others follows a writer and an actor placed under state surveillance in 1984 East Germany. Olivier Award-winner Robert Icke’s production, which runs until early January, stars Knightley alongside Emmy Award-winner Stephen Dillane (Game of Thrones, The Tunnel, Vigil) and Olivier Award-nominee Luke Thompson (Bridgerton, Dunkirk, Oresteia).

#6710. Jacquard Is Having a Moment—Here’s How to Wear It Without Looking Overdone

Statement blazers, muted florals, and metallic touches make jacquard feel modern.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Jacquard is having a moment, and it’s easy to see why. Woven rather than printed, these richly patterned fabrics carry their designs in the very structure of the cloth: florals that rise in subtle relief, geometric motifs that catch the light, tone-on-tone brocades that shimmer when you move. It’s a fabric with the soul of couture and, increasingly, the versatility of everyday dressing.

But a textile this expressive comes with a question every fashion lover eventually asks: how do you wear it without looking like you’ve raided the sofa upholstery?

The answer, as it turns out, is balance. Jacquard works best when it’s allowed to be the star, so the rest of your outfit should play a supporting role. Think a statement blazer paired with clean white tees and straight-leg denim, or a sculptural jacquard miniskirt grounded by a fitted knit and sleek ankle boots. For evenings, a jacquard slip dress needs little more than a sharp jacket and a good pair of heels. Metallic threads and deep jewel tones lean glamorous, while soft neutrals and muted palettes bring a quiet, modern elegance to workwear and weekend looks alike.

Ahead, we’ve gathered some street style inspo that proves jacquard belongs far beyond the red carpet.

Anchored by black leggings and sleek tall boots, this structured jacquard jacket commands the spotlight.

Paired with subtly ruffled denim and a few modern touches, a fitted jacquard blazer brings a hint of Versailles to the street.

Floral jacquard trousers in muted tones prove far more wearable than expected. We love how she paired them with a coordinating knit that echoes the palette without matching the print.

An all-black outfit gets a sophisticated, edgy upgrade with a black and gold jacquard blazer. We love how the mix of sparkly and sculptural jewelry mirrors the metallic shimmer and completes the look.

This skirt and jacket set is a perfect fit for an event or social gathering. The bronze heels provide a striking contrast, while the tiny handbag blends seamlessly into the look.

The contrast of a golden jacquard blazer with an hourglass silhouette against an all-black ensemble proves that a single statement piece can transform an outfit, adding warmth, shine, and a flattering shape without any extra effort.

#6709. All the Best Celebrity Halloween Costumes of 2026

From Sabrina Carpenter as Hairspray’s Penny to Kendall Jenner’s Paris, Texas moment

Another Halloween means another round of celebrity costumes. Every year, actors, artists, and public figures go all out for the holiday, sparing no expense and taking no shortcuts to see their vision through. Whether the stars are sourcing pieces of fashion history, creating custom avant-garde ensembles, or focusing on transforming themselves through hair and makeup, we’ve seen familiar faces turn themselves into, well, unfamiliar faces. And this year has proven no different.

Kicking things off over a week early, Kim Kardashian—who has been known to take the holiday quite seriously, from embracing her law aspirations as Legally Blonde’s Elle Woods or working with artist Alexis Stone on a scaly albino alligator costume—shared photos teasing her 2026 fit on October 20. For this year, she channeled her own daughter North West, and dressed up in the 13-year-old’s signature grunge style (ice-blue wig and all).

Another champion of Halloween, Paris Hilton continued her streak of dressing up as fellow Y2K icon Britney Spears. In past years, she’d done the red leather suit from the “Oops!…I Did It Again” music video, the schoolgirl fit from “...Baby One More Time,” and the flight attendant ensemble from “Toxic,” and here, she went for the iconic 2001 VMAs snake getup.

Elsewhere, Benito Skinner and Owen Thiele went as today’s pop girls; PinkPantheress and Zara Larsson, Cyndi Lauper performed as The Little Mermaid’s Ursula at the New York Restoration Project’s annual Hulaween gala; and Victoria Justice went goth for her own Halloween party.

#6708. What Is Swedish Mys? And Where to Experience It

The brutal cold of Sweden’s winters is well documented, but Stockholm has a knack for bringing some special magic even to its gloomiest season. The city, located just 500 miles south of the Arctic Circle, enjoys as little as six hours of daylight during the bleakest months, with the peak of darkness occurring at the winter solstice on December 21—and it’s no coincidence that this is when Stockholm fills with fairy-lit streets and cozy dining rooms illuminated with candles and roaring fireplaces. Add a whole host of invigorating winter activities, from ice-skating on frozen lakes to plunging through holes drilled in the frozen Baltic Sea, and you’ll see why many Swedes claim this as their favorite time of year.

“Winter is a time to hunker down, cook and play vinyl records; to wear something cozy and breathe the crisp outdoor air; to heat up the sauna, cut a hole in the ice, and—well, don't even get me started on how much I love cold-water plunges!” exclaims Hanna Oscarsson, the creative director at Stockholm’s most celebrated cocktail bar, Röda Huset.

“Cooling off on a hot summer day is wonderful too, of course,” she continues. “But winter dips are like a deep breath to reset the constant, hectic pace of modern life.”

Nowhere does cozy like Sweden—so much so that they even have a special word for it. Mys is Swedish for that feeling you get when you curl up under a soft blanket on a deep-cushioned sofa next to a fireplace, your hands wrapped around a giant cup of hot chocolate, or when you wrap up in your thickest knits and mittens and head out for a bracing walk along winter-lit streets, a soft dusting of snow creaking under your boots. It’s not entirely unrelated to what the Danes call hygge—but where hygge has hardened into an international lifestyle aesthetic, mys remains purely a state of mind.

I grew up in Sweden and toughed it out for 20-or-so years until the darkness got the better of me, prompting me to move to Barcelona and never look back. So imagine my surprise earlier this year, when my feed suddenly filled with Swedish “coolcations”, as the whole of southern Europe fled north to escape the stifling heat. “That’s cool,” I thought to myself, but you don’t really know Sweden until you’ve tasted the cold. So grab your furriest coat and woolliest hat, because when it comes to winter wonderlands, nowhere does it quite like Stockholm.

Imagine jetting off to Stockholm to visit your art collector pals in their private waterfront residence. If an invite from said friends doesn’t come through, a stay at Lydmar Hotel is the next best thing. Part hotel, part gallery, the 46-room property is home to one of the largest, privately owned fine-art photographic collections in the Swedish capital. Each of the plushly upholstered rooms—some of which enjoy unobstructed views of the Stockholm waterfront all the way to the Royal Palace—has its own vibe, brought to life through a curated blend of photography, artworks and bespoke furniture. Expect plenty of mys-filled nooks designed for curling up with a book while gazing at your favorite artwork.

When a hotel set in a former bank names its main dining room Bonnie’s (a tribute to legendary outlaw Bonnie Parker), you know you’re in for a good time. Guests at Bank can warm their cockles at nearby Sturebadet, a beautiful Moorish-inspired Art Nouveau bathhouse and sauna complex (access is included for suite guests while others enjoy preferential rates), before whiling away their evenings in the hotel’s Papillon cocktail lounge, a sultry, wood-paneled affair serving warming Old Fashioneds and Negronis. Spacious rooms come with soft slippers for keeping cozy on icy nights.

Set on the easternmost tip of the well-to-do island of Lidingö (one of the 14 islands that make up the city of Stockholm), Ellery Beach House is a former IBM conference center-turned-Palm-Springs-inspired seaside fun palace. If toasty saunas and invigorating sea dunks are your poison, you’ll be in your element in the hotel’s four heated pools and two saunas, one of which is set right on the edge of the Baltic Sea—ideal for defrosting after a glacial plunge. There are countless other activities on offer, from padel to cornhole or boules, to sipping cocktails under a blanket in the firepit-heated relaxation room.

Swedish chef Niklas Ekstedt uses only natural heat, soot, ash, smoke, and fire to prepare the tasting menu at his eponymous Michelin-starred restaurant. This is a meal that will stay with you long after you’ve trudged home through the snowy streets, frost-nipped gloved fingertips in pockets and earlobes tucked under woolly hats. You’ll sample the first courses, such as the Swedish caviar, or vendace roe, topped with crown dill, and be served a cast-iron flatbread in the snug, flame-heated kitchen, before being led to a table for the mains, which include a sublime confit North Sea langoustine, like a smaller, firmer lobster, infused with rose and lingonberries.

Nowhere does mys like this comfy café set in a castle-like structure built for the 1897 Stockholm World’s Fair. It makes a perfect stop for oversized cups of tea and fresh-baked cinnamon rolls in front of the crackling fireplace after a bracing walk along the Djurgården shoreline, past the Kärleksudden (Lovers’ Point) observation deck with its breathtaking views.

Where better to sample a Swedish classic than this low-lit space tucked alongside the Stockholm Opera House? Bakfickan (the back pocket) is an intimate, 28-seater restaurant that serves Swedish comfort food, or husmanskost, including their best-selling meatballs with all the customary accoutrements: creamy gravy, potato purée, lingonberries, and pickled cucumber.

The name Röda Huset (red house) conjures images of a red-painted cottage deep in the Swedish countryside, but this particular house is as urban as it gets, with its bright crimson façade overlooking Sergels Torg, Stockholm’s most central square. The moodily lit top floor is home to an award-winning cocktail lounge, where drinks are laced with hyperlocal Scandinavian produce. Think a Manhattan infused with heather honey from the Baltic island of Gotland or a tart, green tomato-flavored Dry Martini.

Stockholm’s southside, or Södermalm (Söder for short), was once an impoverished district where affordable homes were built for factory workers. These days, it’s best known for its laid-back, unbothered vibe, lively bars, and hip stores. It’s where Swedish actor Alexander Skarsgård grew up and where you’ll often spot him casually hanging out with friends when he’s in town. Start at Nytorget and wander through the trendy SoFo district, where you can marvel at the Christmas lights and the elegant Nordic Classicism buildings. Once you reach Mariatorget, climb the hill to Monteliusgatan Street where you’ll be treated to one of the most magnificent aerial views across the frozen waters of Riddarfjärden to the snow-dusted rooftops of Stockholm City Hall.

Staying in Söder, the area around Nytorget is full of chic boutiques like the 6/5/4 clothing and concept store and menswear brand A Day’s March (to help you perfect that effortlessly cool Skarsgård look). Don’t miss interior design mecca Tambur, where you can invest in some mys of your own, like cozy wool throws and charcoal-scented candles. Meanwhile, Hornsgatan is home to the Slow Fashion District, a collection of some 20 stores selling vintage, second-hand, upcycled and sustainable fashions, as well as skincare and homewares.

Swedes love to tell you “there’s no such thing as bad weather, only bad clothing”—and winter is the time to prove them right as you bundle up and get your outdoor mys on. It takes a cold winter for Stockholm’s waterways to freeze over, but when they do, they fill with ice skaters from families teaching their little ones to stay upright to hardcore long-distance skaters. If open water ice-skating is too adventurous, there’s also a public rink in central Stockholm at Kungsträdgården. Meanwhile, winter kayaking is an exhilarating option for milder winters.

Saunas are as beloved here as they are in Finland, but scoring a membership at one of Stockholm’s private sauna clubs is like getting an audience with His Majesty King Carl XVI Gustaf. This year saw the launch of Stockholm’s first-ever public sauna, Bastun Tanto: a photogenic, verdigris-green floating pontoon set on the fashionable Hornstull waterfront that offers icy plunges and steamy warm-ups all winter long (with slots bookable 30 days in advance).

Inside Rebecca Hall and Morgan Spector’s Art-Filled Hudson Valley Home

Sterling Point’s Lakeside Filming Locations Are the Ultimate Fall Getaway

Meet the Women Leading Norway’s Seaweed Revolution

#6706. The 2026 GQ Grooming Awards

How far will men go to look better? From peptides to eye-color-change surgeries, 2026 has familiarized us with an ever-growing list of extreme methods for self-improvement. But sometimes the simplest things can make the biggest difference: A silky face cream, a reliable hair mousse, or an irresistible fragrance can go a long way toward boosting your confidence and, in the process, making you feel your best. No invasive procedures or experimental injections necessary.

We at the GQ offices are constantly testing new products, and over the course of 2026 we’ve slathered on many an antiaging moisturizer, smoothed our jaws with a Jetsons-level gua sha, and snipped our body hair with highly precise trimmers. We also scoured all corners of the grooming world, from a face wash made on a 13th-century Tuscan estate to an odor-eliminating hand wash developed by Michelin-starred chefs in Los Angeles. The result is a comprehensive list of stylish, unique goops, sprays, foams, and wellness tech worthy of your medicine cabinet.

Our criteria for the GQ Grooming Awards remains the same as it has been in the decade-plus that we’ve been testing products: Innovation and efficacy were paramount, followed by ease of use and intuitive, stylish packaging. For the most part we kept our choices limited to new releases, with a few small exceptions for less novel products we just couldn’t put down. The following guide also offers a wide range of price points—from low-key, affordable drugstore staples to high-end cult faves worth splurging on or gifting. Grooming shouldn’t be a constant practice of self-correction. It can be a small daily ritual that allows for playfulness, discovery, and joy. Go ahead and get your feet wet. —Tyler Chin

Vanicream is cool because it’s not trying to be cool. The famously no-frills brand is a drugstore classic and favorite among skin-care savants, historically known as a go-to face-and-body lotion for especially sensitive skin. Its new Enhanced Moisturizer gives that formula a beefy upgrade, chock-full of five (!) ceramides to support the skin barrier and hyaluronic acid and glycerin to deliver hydration. The whopping 12-ounce pump means you can use it from face to body without feeling like you need to ration every drop. —Michael Stefanov

Think of Aestura as the Korean La Roche-Posay: derm-minded, hip, and already tried-and-trusted. Its new Hydro Soothing Cream does something rare in the world of skin care: It delivers lasting hydration in a cooling, near-weightless formula that includes ceramides and hyaluronic acid to support the skin barrier and zinc to keep excess oil in check. The gel-cream texture feels silky on contact, and offers long-lasting reults: After applying this in the morning, my skin still feels hydrated by midday. —M.S.

A clever toner pad that doubles as a five-minute face mask and cooling hydrator? Yes, please. Biodance’s Caviar PDRN Dual Gel Toner Pads have a two-sided design: Sweep the soft cotton side, soaked in a gently exfoliating milky serum, over your skin, then press the cool, squishy hydrogel side anywhere else you want an extra hit of hydration. Leave it for 5 to 10 minutes, and it essentially becomes a mini targeted sheet mask. The caviar-and-salmon PDRN-infused formula left my face softer, plumper, and more hydrated. These are just so darn refreshing to use. —M.S.

K-beauty power player Sulwhasoo’s new Essential Revitalizing Water Ex is a far cry from the clinical, seemingly extraneous toners many of us grew up with. Its silky, slightly viscous water-gel texture feels soothing and hydrating without adding any weight, which is why on warmer days I may sometimes skip a moisturizer after using this. I’ve noticed that the addition of herbs, plum blossom, and antioxidants helps to calm my skin after applying the toner, which is great because those ingredients are meant to support skin health. —M.S.

There are all types of cleansers out there: foam, gel, clay, balm—you name it. But I’m not sure we’ve come across a texture quite like Fresh’s Soy Cleansing Jelly Balm. Its cool, translucent “oil-in-jelly” formula starts thick, cushiony, and, well, jelly-like, then turns silky as you massage it in. Add water, and it emulsifies into a light serum that cuts through stubborn SPF, excess oil, and city grime like a champ, then rinses clean without a greasy residue. An excellent nighttime reset for the sunscreen-obsessives and/or city-dwellers among us. —M.S.

Hero’s Gentle Cleanser leaves your skin feeling clean without making it tight and dry, thanks to aloe and cica. The latter, a nickname for Centella asiatica, is a potent ingredient that calms and heals the skin despite the fact that the rest of the product is doing its job to wash away the dirt and grime that your face has accumulated during the day. After using it my skin felt restored and supple—proof that harsh face washes aren’t always necessary to do the job. —T.C.

Conceived of in a state-of-the-art lab on a 13th-century Tuscan estate, Seed To Skin’s Rescue Cleanse is a cut above your average face cleanser. It comes packaged in a stylish metal tube, smells faintly of rosemary, and has a silky, goop-like texture somewhere between whipped cream and olive oil. Most crucially, when you rub the glycerin wash over your face and then splash water, it transforms into a subtle balm that simultaneously removes excess oil and nourishes your skin. It is inherently gentle, great to use for every skin type, but especially welcome for anyone suffering from rosacea. I’ve found I gravitate toward it most during both Los Angeles’s windy winters and especially dry summer months, where a lot of other cleansers that contain common alcohols, phthalates, or sulfates would completely disrupt my skin barrier and cause dry patches. At $128 it’s a higher-end product, but one you will luxuriate in at every use. Leave it to the Italians to understand that, as long as you’re working with high-quality ingredients, less is more. —Alyssa Bereznak

It’s hard to keep track of all La Roche-Posay’s stellar face washes, but the new Moisturizing Milky Cleanser deserves all the smoke this year. Its pared-down formula has just nine ingredients, which are focused on cleansing and hydration. And the fragrance-free, milky texture produces a creamy consistency that feels far more luxurious than its drugstore price would suggest. It’s especially effective for those with dry or sensitive skin, but it’s honestly a really solid product for anyone to reach for, morning or evening. —M.S.

Some exfoliants take an everything-but-the-kitchen-sink approach, but Reale Actives gets more targeted with Go Deep, formulated to help keep breakouts in check while smoothing overall skin texture. The serum is powered by mandelic acid, a gentler exfoliating acid that works more gradually, making it a solid choice for acne-prone or sensitive skin. Like most serums, use this after your toner and before your heavier moisturizer. When I add a few drops of this to my skin, it absorbs quickly without any sticky residue. What a relief that we can leave the days of nuclear acne treatments behind us for something more elevated. —M.S.

Don’t let the “peel” in the name throw you off: G.M. Collin’s new exfoliating toner is surprisingly easygoing. There’s no gritty physical exfoliation here; the watery formula combines gentle AHAs, BHA, and PHA to improve texture and tackle clogged pores without feeling like I sandblasted my face. It gives a thrilling little tingle before leaving my mug feeling smooth and renewed. Use it morning and night, though I recommend you ease into it, especially if you already use retinoids or other hardworking actives. —M.S.

Few brands do au naturel quite as well as Ursa Major, with ingredient lists that sound more like the spoils of a foraging party than those of a skincare product. But there’s plenty of science behind their concoctions too. When the brand launched its first-ever eye treatment this year, our perennial eye bags perked up. Sunny Start pairs tried-and-true depuffing caffeine with fine-line-smoothing niacinamide, brightening vitamin C, and the usual bounty of botanicals like sandalwood and rosehip seed oil. The texture is more barely there serum than traditional eye cream, calming on contact before sinking in quickly. Read: no gunk. A sunny start to your morning, indeed. —M.S.

They call it a serum, but this is more of a hydrating face oil, and an elevated, earthy one at that. An array of potent botanical oils nourishes and softens the skin, and it smells great: Frankincense, thyme, and lavender give it a distinctly herbaceous quality, like the formula was freshly distilled from bark and leaves. A few drops left my skin soft, supple, and glowy. Face oils can sometimes veer too granola or overly chemical, but I find this one hits the sweet spot. —M.S.

In both my lifelong quest to maintain a glowing complexion and in my role as GQ’s wellness and grooming director, I have probably slathered on enough retinol to cover the entirety of Rhode Island (or at least, like, Providence.) All that to say, I know a really effective, innovative entry into the retinol category when I see one. Mother Science is a brand founded by Incubus guitarist Mike Einziger and his partner Ann Marie Simpson-Einziger. Their idea for the brand came about after Simpson-Einziger developed a mysterious fungal infection and went on a quest to better understand why it was happening. During that journey she worked in tandem with professionals to determine that a molecule called malassezin is beneficial for reducing sun spots. And thus was the beginning of a product line that has done more to even out my skin tone than any other I’ve tried before. Their Retinol Synergist serum, which came out in 2024, is a one-two punch of skin renewal and wrinkle reduction (courtesy of the malassezin). Since I began using it earlier this year, I can’t bring myself to buy anything else. And since my fiancé discovered how good it is, I’ve had to share my bottle. Pro tip: They sell it at Sephora now. —A.B.

It’s almost surprising that Aesop took this long to make a retinoid, but the brand’s first foray into the category is exactly what you’d hope for: effective, gentle, and with that trademark elevated botanical aura. A next-gen vitamin A derivative targets fine lines, uneven tone, and texture with less potential for irritation than your average retinol. At $179, it’s nothing to sneeze at, but few retinoids we’ve tested feel this elevated or downright pleasurable to apply. Think of it as antiaging meets aromatherapy. —M.S.

For the uninitiated, gua sha is an ancient Chinese practice that uses a smooth-edged tool to stroke across the skin, ease tension, and temporarily depuff one’s lymph nodes. Ora Method modernizes the ritual with red- and near-infrared-light therapy and gentle vibration for a three-in-one treatment. Glide its U-shaped contouring edge along your jaw, cheeks, forehead, and neck for immediate massage-and-depuff gratification, while the red- and infrared-light work on wrinkles over time. —M.S.

I fully believe in the power of an LED mask, and it’s why I never hesitated to include it as an add-on during my facials. In my years of trying out red-light face masks, Currentbody has always been a brand I return to. Now in its third iteration, this is maybe the most innovative version yet. Approved by the FDA, the face mask offers six different wavelengths to target various skin concerns like wrinkles, acne, and hyperpigmentation. Like other LED masks, that’s a given. What’s new is that this mask emits those wavelengths at what the brand says is just the right dosage for whatever your skin-care goals might be. Once you strap into the mask, you can choose from four preset modes: advanced antiaging, advanced clearing, complexion corrector, and balance and brighten. Once you get the hang of that and learn what the various colored wavelengths do for your skin, you can customize your own treatments via the new Currentbody app. The interface allows you to activate different lights across the face mask so you can address whatever skin concerns you may have anywhere on your mug: like if you want to minimize a zit on your nose while simultaneously smoothing out the lines on your forehead. Listen, results don’t happen overnight (or even in a matter of weeks), but consistent use of the Series 3 does make me feel like there’s a better reflection of myself looking back at me in the mirror. —T.C.

The price tag is steep, but the results glow for themselves. Here, vitamin C—in all its brightening, antioxidant goodness—is the star, alongside niacinamide and the brand’s signature TFC8 technology, a blend of amino acids and vitamins that promote cell renewal (a.k.a. fresh skin turnover). This isn’t a watery serum; it has a cushiony texture that melts into the skin with a velvety finish, leaving it oh so soft and hydrated. With continued use, I noticed fewer fine lines and a more even tone, without any of the irritation that can sometimes accompany active-heavy formulas. —M.S.

No, Kiehl’s hasn’t gotten into the injectable business. But the OG apothecary brand is zeroing in on more high-performance skin care, and its new serum brings back serious bounce to saggy skin. The hotshot formula combines collagen polypeptides and vitamin B12 in a silky serum that gave my skin an almost instant glow-up. —M.S.

The skincareification of lips is here. And it’s about time. This Obagi treatment is essentially a serum, balm, and sunscreen in one, stacking plumping peptides, hydrating hyaluronic acid, soothing calendula, and SPF 30. Its long-lasting, Vaseline-like texture coated my lips with a protective barrier sans much sheen while helping to smooth fine lines along the way, with a subtle tingle to boot. In other words, it hydrates, treats, and protects your lips. There’s not much more we can ask for than that. Lip balm of the year. —M.S.

Leave it to concentrated-tincture kings Typology to think outside the box with this treatment-meets-patch setup. It works like this: First, you smooth on a cooling cream with a 7% peptide complex to help firm and fill in lines around the eyes, forehead, and mouth, and then you seal it in with reusable silicone patches. It seemed a bit gimmicky at first, but when I peeled the patches off after 10 minutes, the area around my eyes looked smoother and brighter. That immediate gratification, plus a reusable design that feels far less wasteful than your average sheet mask, makes this item one of the year’s smartest skin-care innovations. —M.S.

Mineral sunscreen has a reputation for being sort of chalky, but Korres’s new SPF 50 is anything but. The fluid lotion is silky and featherlight, spreading quickly and sinking in rather than sitting on the skin. And the IYKYK Greek skin-care brand puts its cred to work too: Alongside 10% zinc oxide and niacinamide, its signature Greek yogurt powder nourishes and heals the skin thanks to its high-protein content. It’s sunscreen playing double agent as skin care. SPF should be a non-negotiable daily habit, and the pleasure I get from using this Korres product gives me real incentive to stick to it. —M.S.

This Tokyo-based skin-care brand makes some of my favorite serums and moisturizers, and its body wash is a blessing for those with eczema-prone and/or sensitive skin. Shea butter reduces moisture loss, which is common with body washes, and olive fruit and jojoba seed oils rehydrate the skin even after you’re done rinsing away the soapy lather. With fragrance notes of minty shiso, the wash foams well with only a nickel-size dollop. I’m usually fragrance-averse because scents can dry out or irritate my skin, but this one hasn’t had that effect. —Leon Hedgepeth

For those who prefer old-fashioned bar soaps over body washes, a block from Paris-based Marc Antoine Barrois is the holy grail. The maison is known for its highly sought-after fragrances, which have now been infused into a range of soaps. Among the five variations the brand offers, Encelade stands out. The preservative-free slab has notes of rhubarb, vetiver, and leather, which linger on your skin long after you’ve rinsed away the suds. You’ll emerge from your morning shower feeling clean but not dried out, courtesy of sunflower seed oil and vitamin E. —L.H.

Let’s get one thing straight: Aging is a privilege. But even so, there’s no shame in using products that help you do it gracefully. This serum from Plated, which is meant to be applied anywhere on the body where there’s thinning or sagging (like the chest, abdomen, or neck), does exactly that. Exosomes in the product help skin cells boost collagen, improve elasticity, and even out the skin’s complexion. I’ve been incorporating it into my evening routine so the serum can penetrate overnight and my skin looks brighter and more hydrated. —L.H.

Founded by Pharrell in 2020, skin-care brand Humanrace uses dermatologist-backed formulations in easy-to-use, minimalist packaging. This fragrance-free body lotion features magnesium, which can support recovery from muscle soreness and sleep. I’ve been using it after I get out of the shower and must say: It really is lightweight and fast-absorbing. Soreness from simply being a dad does seem to go away faster when I incorporate this into my regimen. —L.H.

When the season changes and the air gets a bit cooler, it’s beneficial to incorporate a body oil as an additional barrier before lotion. This one has ingredients like macadamia oil, squalene, and vitamin E. The brand offers two scent options: santal and vetiver, which conjures up images of a roaring campfire; and bergamot and hinoki, a tart and energizing hit of citrus. A little goes a long way, and I’ve been using a dab around the neck area post-shave to prevent razor burn. —L.H.

Diptyque’s personal-care products are now renowned in their own right, alongside the Parisian house’s famed colognes and candles. The brand’s new Perfecting Satin Oil takes the mess out of body oil: Mist it straight onto your skin after a shower for a hydrating subtle sheen. It does hair too: Apply it through the ends for extra softness, shine, or moisture—especially clutch in winter’s dry, frigid months. And, of course, being Diptyque, it smells fantastic; the light scent of jasmine and warm vanilla idle wherever you apply it. —M.S.

This kitschy tub of body scrub is an effective way to exfoliate for a variety of different skin types. (It’s even gentle enough for my five-year-old son.) The raw sugar and vitamin C slough away dead skin, while the coconut oil and maple leave my skin feeling renewed post-spritz. This is one we’ll always keep in stock, especially once winter approaches. I can feel the hydration in my skin even days after using it, and it doesn’t make the shower floor slippery like most vitamin E body scrubs. —L.H.

Naomi is an LA-based brand created by cooks for cooks, whose hands must be vigorously washed after almost every task. Their hand wash smells of delicate California sage and blood orange, meant to combat whatever odors may stick to one’s paws after chopping pungent ingredients like garlic or onions. The secret is enzymes derived from saccharomyces, which is the same yeast that’s used in making bread and brewing beer. It effectively breaks down the stinky molecules that don’t get washed away with standard hand washes. The key hydrating ingredients are extracts from prickly pear and fig leaf, which are native to the city the brand hails from. Plus the amber color makes me feel like I’m cleaning my hands with honey. —L.H.

Medicube’s deodorizing stick is alcohol- and aluminum-free and has a gourmand scent of vanilla and pistachio that conjures up memories of sunscreen and piña coladas. I’m wary of trying new deodorants because I’m prone to an underarm rash, but this one has caused no such thing. I’ll do about six swipes under each arm daily, and the formula with turmeric, vitamin C, and niacinamide is gentle enough for even the most sensitive pits. —L.H.

In recent years, I’ve come to terms with the fact that my hands deserve the same bedtime routine as my face. Just like the skin on your face, hands age at a fast rate, but they aren’t afforded the same care and attention to keep them looking young. For the last few months, I’ve been applying Aesop’s hand serum every night before bed and noticed that my skin feels plumper and brighter, a.k.a. the opposite of the crepey texture, wrinkles, and dark spots typically associated with older hands. It’s like the step in your facial routine where you apply a serum before you add your moisturizer: Add one pump to the back of both hands, rub together, and then follow it up with your preferred hand balm or body lotion. I’ve struggled with rough and dry hands from an early age, but since using this they’ve become soft and smooth, all without a greasy feeling. Packed with ingredients like dandelion root, niacinamide, and LHA, the serum also has a subtle floral scent that doesn’t feel overpowering. —L.H.

Part fragrance, part deodorant, AKT’s Body Mist is a far cry from the overly potent locker room sprays you used after PE class. The British brand is headed by two cofounders who were West End theater actors seeking to make deodorizing products that wouldn’t let sweat sully their stage performances. This mist works to neutralize body odors without irritating the skin. And it’s versatile enough to come in handy for everything from the finishing touch of your morning grooming routine to a post-gym spritz. There are five scents to choose from, all of which leave a low-key aroma that’s soft on the nose but big on counteracting unwanted smells. Given my preference toward bergamot, I particularly liked the Orange Grove scent, which leans more bitter citrus zest than sweet Fanta. —T.C.

As a curly-haired guy, I found that Stmnt’s Hydro shampoo left my locks feeling bouncy and smooth with just a couple of pumps. It’s probably because the brand made sure to eschew the typical sulfates and silicone oils that are added to shampoos, which help with lathering and giving your hair that silky feel afterward but ultimately irritate the scalp and strip away too much of the hair’s natural oils. Instead, the addition of ginseng extract and bisabolol alleviate any scalp irritation, and it’s all complemented by the wonderful scent of lavender blended with tonka bean. —L.H.

This grooming upstart from Boulder has been a Grooming Awards recipient for a few years now, and its new conditioner continues with its winning streak. A lighter-weight formula, it retains the hair’s natural movement and doesn’t make it feel weighed down, as heavier conditioners tend to do. Despite its more liquid consistency, four to five pumps still made my hair feel soft, detangled, and, well, conditioned. The rosemary fragrance smells fresh, especially with a warm shower, and the mango seed oil and argan oil left my hair looking shiny but not greasy. —L.H.

As men’s hair trends toward longer styles, looser hair-styling products have become a necessity. This year, Firsthand added Air Creme to its existing collection of pomades to fill that need. It’s a lightweight styler that doubles as a moisturizer for your hair while doing its intended purpose of taming it. I’ve been using this product on the daily to style my hair, which feels light and free but still retains the shape that I want throughout the day. —T.C.

There’s nothing wrong with losing your hair, but that doesn’t mean you can’t also be proactive in keeping what you have left. Though this Sisley hair serum won’t turn your dome into a Chia Pet overnight, using it consistently will thicken your mane within about a month. The secret sauce is a cocktail of good-for-your hair ingredients like rice proteins for regenerating hair follicles, einkorn wheat extract for strengthening the root, ginger extract for reducing scalp irritation, and a healthy dose of vitamins and minerals to promote hair strength from the inside out. I’ve already noticed fewer hairs in the drain since using it, and incorporating it into the post-morning shower routine makes it a small, hassle-free step with a big reward. I’m not actively worried about losing my hair but, hey, a preventative step like this is a great first gesture toward maintaining that hairline before I consider oral supplements. —T.C.

Roz, founded by celebrity hairstylist Mara Roszak, created a mousse that’s designed to deliver definition, volume, and flexible hold without the dreaded crunch we’ve come to expect from the product category. Hydrating ingredients like hyaluronic acid and glycerin help keep strands feeling conditioned, while styling polymers provide lasting shape and control. It also enhances natural curls and waves. As someone with straight hair, I’ve noticed it makes my hair soft and touchable with an added oomph that I don’t usually get from other products. —T.C.

The skin-scent stalwarts at Escentric Molecules have been on a streak lately, and its latest ’fume riffs on a slept-on note: clary sage. The German house pairs the calming, aromatic herb with its iconic Molecule 01, that low-grade skin-scent veil of Iso E Super. Clary sage makes the mixture smell slightly floral and a little spicy, while Molecule 01 keeps it smooth and woody. The result is subtle, with an almost aromatherapy-like quality: interesting enough that your water cooler colleague will inquire, but not loud enough to announce your arrival. —M.S.

Boss has thrown enough curveballs recently to keep us scent aficionados paying attention. Bottled Beyond EDT Intense is the latest, built around the intriguing dichotomy of bright orange blossom and warm leather. The orange blossom is crisp, luminous, and almost sparkling, while the leather adds sensual depth. That duality between clean and carnal gives it a magnetic tension without losing the easygoing, compliment-friendly quality Boss does so well. It’s polished enough for the office, sexy enough for after hours, and has enough panache to become an everyday signature scent. —M.S.

Few noses bring the same luxurious, impossibly chic sensibility to oud as Mr. Kurkdjian himself. The brand’s already released a few scents under this category, but Oud Velvet Mood sticks out from the bunch. In fact, this might be FK’s best variation yet. It’s fall-appropriate given its leathery and spicy backbone, losing the more usual sweet notes that you might typically associate with an oud. And because of that, the scent speaks to the possibilities of where a cooler evening might lead you. Only a handful of notes comprise this gem: a heady dose of oud warmed with spices and saffron, then softened by a resinous balm. Moody, velvety, and transportive, it’s a starry-night scent that practically begs to be worn on your date nights. —M.S.

We wouldn’t fault you for thinking the new Reserve is an attempt to capitalize on the popularity of Ombré Leather with another flanker (at least its name doesn’t contain the word Extreme). But this update is anything but. Tom Ford takes its signature Leather West, surrounding it with juniper, cypress, black pepper, and dry woods for something smokier, more resinous, and more aromatic than the OG: less sleek biker jacket, more rustic brown leather in the desert. It’s sexy, sultry, very elegantly Tom Ford, but also approachable, with plenty of staying power to boot. One hell of a fragrance for a night out at a bar, a rodeo, or just a rodeo-themed bar. —M.S.

Decision 42, a remix of the cult-classic Decision, smells like the most luxurious evergreen forest you’ve ever encountered, an intoxicating mix of pungent woods, spice, and sweet maple sap. But this, my friends, is Amouage, so there are twists and turns abound: Pink pepper, cinnamon, frankincense, myrrh, neroli, and patchouli weave in and out of this regal and oh so rich scent. From the impossibly gorgeous bottle to the whopping concentration, everything about Decision 42 feels like an event, which is exactly why I’d reserve this one for special occasions. —M.S.

A new Creed drop always warrants interest from olfactory nerds like myself, and Iris Debonair is exactly what its name suggests: dashing. Its green verdance, like a bundle of dried herbs, is paired with a heady, effusive iris that conjures old-world French elegance, while bergamot, lavender, and amber add a hit of brightness that makes for a rainbow of playful scent notes. —M.S.

Future Memories captures that unmistakable Byredo signature—a lemony, incensey skin-scent undertone—but layers in a gorgeous gourmand, taking vanilla in a decidedly less cloying direction. This is a theme we’re seeing in fragrance lately, but Future Memories is one of the best variations of it, period. Black currant and aldehydes give the mixture a tart freshness, while iris, labdanum, and orange blossom conjure a glowing, flower-filled cathedral before a boozy vanilla-liqueur accord warms the senses. The scent is vanilla, yes, but a sophisticated, very wearable take that keeps you guessing. It feels nostalgic and futuristic at the same time. —M.S.

French modern-grooming legend Horace has become a favorite of ours because of its approachable price point for scents that can stand alongside the pricier heavy hitters. Ibiza ’87, much like Vintage Vanilla before it, smells nothing like its name suggests. One might expect an EDM-ridden, sun-drenched beach soiree, but instead the scent is imbued with gorgeous woody aromatics that shift the celebration to holiday territory. Fir and balsam give it a crisp, Christmassy backbone, herbal undertones keep it fresh, and a Champagne note adds just a bit of sparkle. It’s easy to wear and pretty easy on the wallet too. (Despite smelling like you spent considerably more.) —M.S.

If an authentic, elegant rose is your speed, this newer Dior Privée fragrance is a true-to-form take on the flower. Rose Star centers on centifolia and damask roses, but instead of going powdery or jammy, it radiates a fresher, greener edge with creamy lushness underneath. Zesty fruit swings between lychee, raspberry, and pear, while Sichuan pepper adds bite and honeyed musk brings some skin-scent sensuality. It’s sweet, a bit spicy, and unmistakably rosy, but still light enough for spring days and cooler summer nights. —M.S.

The ’90s nostalgia train shows no signs of slowing down, and if you were around for one of the best cultural decades of all time you know the Gap fragrance line was legendary. Gap released five new scents this year as an homage to that era, reimagined for today, and Grass is a throwback in the best way. The mall brand seemingly bottled up what it’s like to go for a balmy morning walk when the dew has settled on suburban lawns. Add some crisp mandarin, matcha, and cedar, and you have yourself a winning combination that evokes memories of some of the best pre-smartphone spring and summer days. It rocks as an easygoing spritzer, all with a very ’90s price tag. Grunge flannel not included. —M.S.

The inimitable French house does so many genres well, all with the chic fougère sophistication PDM is known for, but my favorites lean into rich woods. Hot off the press for fall, Dorval balances smoky guaiac wood and warm cinnamon with clary sage for just enough of that old-school cologne lean. It’s warm, enveloping, and oh so sexy—a scent that will earn you more than one What are you wearing? —M.S.

Ambre Levant, with its hushed whispers on fragrance forums, seems destined to join the house’s bona fide olfactory classics. Amber is the operative word here, and it absolutely glows while surrounded by a hefty dose of oud and a glimmer of spicy cinnamon. There’s little brightness to speak of; this is heady business, with a dark, almost funky edge from the oud. It’s not one for fragrance novices, and you’ll want to cap yourself at two sprays max, but the effect is intoxicating. Ambre Levant radiates off the skin like a warm light in the winter night, leaving a rich, ambery veil in its wake. —M.S.

Whether you wanna look like Gandalf or a clean-shaven hobbit, this Norelco’s got you. I found the amount of blades to be an embarrassment of riches, ranging in different widths (for hyper-specific cuts) to a variety of length options. The best part? It’s a trimmer that actually catches the hair it shaves off your face, so it doesn’t spread all over your sink. —R.L.

Bic was the first company to release a one-piece razor, a breakthrough for being affordable and disposable. Its Flex 5 is making even more waves in the category. The kit comes with multiple razors and an array of clip-ons to achieve not just your desired five o’clock shadow, but any other time of shade you’d want. At one point, I used it to go at my stubble sans shaving cream, and my skin didn’t suffer in the slightest. Even better? For everything you get, the Flex 5’s price point is its sharpest attribute. —R.L.

The Nevo is the Lamborghini of electric shavers. Braun’s decked it out with every bell and whistle from 250 diamond sharp blades, an automatic sensor that gauges the thickness of your beard to adjust its power, and a slick swivel that adjusts to the curves of any face. One of the most tedious parts of shaving is adjusting your movements to go with the direction of your facial hair’s growth. The Nevo is able to collect hair growing in multiple directions so that you can do fewer swipes across your face for a close-as-hell shave that rivals that of a manual razor. My resulting baby face is a testament to this bad boy’s impressive technological feat. —R.L.

Whether we’re talking about Jason Momoa or Nick Offerman, a groomed beard can be a man’s pièce de résistance. In applying this to my hairy mug, the dropper allowed me to deliver nourishment straight to the skin and follicle, not just the top of my beard. There’s only a hint of aroma from the oil, though I wouldn’t mind if its fresh citrusy scent were actually a little more prominent—that’s just how good it smells. —R.L.

You don’t use shampoo on your beard because the skin under your beard requires something more gentle than your scalp. Manscaped’s beard wash does an excellent job at cleaning your whiskers without drying out the skin. The brand incorporated ceramides and avocado oil into the concoction, which sort of makes this a souped-up face wash that’s also great at cleansing your scruff. It’s a delightful formula that freshens up the forest on your face and makes the extra grooming step feel more like self-care than burden. —R.L.

On slow mornings, there’s nothing more relaxing than taking your time with a crisp shave. Cue grooming stalwart Malin + Goetz to up the zen ante. The addition of chamomile does more than create a nice, subtle flowery scent, it also helps to nourish the skin for what it’s about to endure. Breathe in as you put on the easy-to-apply, textured cream. Breathe out as you glide your razor of choice, leaving behind a delightful landscape of bare skin that’s not the least bit irritated. —R.L.

Made by barbers, Stmnt has become a new standard in men’s grooming. The brand utilizes high-quality ingredients in its products and prioritizes taking care of not just your hair but the skin underneath. Its Aftershave Lotion combines vitamin E and aloe vera along with allantoin and panthenol to aid in the healing process post-shave. —R.L.

Professional barbers across this great nation turn to Andis for all of their hair-taming needs. But thankfully I did not need to go to beauty school to use the company’s Zephyr Clipper, a neat cordless device with two speed settings and a two-hour battery life. It’s ideal for everything from slight trims to major shaves, and it’s a great way to prolong a haircut while you’re waiting for your next barber appointment. —R.L.

Body hair can be a blessing—just ask all the celebs showing off their chest hair this year— but it’s also gotta be tamed. Marlowe’s simple Body Trimmer is a no-frills, exacting way to cut all of your hedges. Its lightweight design makes it easy to travel with. I’ve personally brought it on beach getaways where I’ve primped everything from my nether regions to my pesky shoulder hair. Instead of acting like a lawn mower ripping through everything, Marlowe’s handy device is much more precise. Double-added bonus: It’s both rechargeable and waterproof. —R.L.

The worst thing you can do when you’re trimming your down-there region is to cut more than just your hair. To protect your delicate skin, Gillette designed its blades to be closer together than those you’d find on a shaver for your face. In doing so, it reduces the risk of skin bulging so that you’re less likely to nick yourself. Remember that this is just a trimmer so you won’t be completely bare, but you’ll get yourself a close-enough shave without the risk of injury. —R.L.

Founded by toothpaste obsessive Eric Buss, the 11-year-old California-based, family-owned brand gives the big boys in oral hygiene a run for their money. The Hydroxi Toothpaste’s highlight is the use of nano-hydroxyapatite. It’s a mineral that’s similar to that found in your enamel, and that’s particularly good for slowing down and reversing early tooth decay. Plus it helps to brighten even the most caffeine-stained chompers without the usual stinging you’d get from more aggressive whitening toothpastes. —R.L.

If all you know is drugstore-brand mouthwashes, it’s time to open up your world to new possibilities. While those burn your mouth or blast it with intensity, Boka’s Renew Mouthwash offers a deliciously subtle and souped-up alternative. The nuanced combination of mint and lemongrass blend into an extremely chill yet yummy swish. (Though you still shouldn’t swallow.) That’s probably because, in lieu of alcohol, it’s actually made with xylitol and bills itself as a pH-balanced prebiotic rinse. Added bonus: the sleek lime-green bottle sits pretty in the bathroom. —R.L.

No matter how many times my dentist scolds me for not flossing, I kick the good oral hygiene habit a few weeks after every visit. Twice-daily brushing I can get behind, but flossing, unlike plaque, just never sticks. Dyson’s CameraJet ensures that I can knock out both with one two-minute session. The new personal grooming tool from the brand that revolutionized cordless stick vacs is a very good electric toothbrush with a couple added gimmicks that are actually pretty cool. For one, the toothbrush has a tiny little camera in it that lets you see inside your mouth (so you can make sure you’re not missing any spots). For another, it identifies debris or gaps between the teeth that could benefit from a targeted blast of water. Can it get a little messy? Sure, the first couple of brushes take some getting used to, but carefully adjusting your brushing movements—like keeping your mouth closed while getting after those molars—reduces the risk of medicine cabinet splashage. And if at my next appointment my doc asks if I’m flossing, I’ll have the video footage to prove it. —T.C.

[Editor’s Note: Following the CameraJet's initial September 8 release, Dyson took a “temporary out-of-stock position” on the CameraJet over an undisclosed “component issue,” according to the brand. (Meaning it was no longer available to buy.) Some early testers have said the toothbrush stopped working shortly after first using it, but we have not experienced this problem. The brand told us that it expects to restock the CameraJet in December.]

Sometimes more is actually better, and when it comes to the SuperBrush that is actually true. The brush contains more bristles—over 10,000 to be exact—than a conventional toothbrush, and it organizes said bristles into two concentric circles that make better contact with the teeth and gumline for improved brushing. Using it made my teeth feel squeaky clean because of how well it reaches areas that my previous toothbrush couldn’t. —R.L.

It’s about time that flossing entered the future. It wasn’t long ago that we struggled with long, gunky strings that stuck to the side of our bathroom trash can. There’s also those rudimentary single-use plastic contraptions that feel excessive and wasteful. Meanwhile the proud Slate Origin Electric Flosser takes all of the hallmarks of an electric toothbrush (including humming vibration) and translates them to this sleek piece of gum-cleansing machinery. Along with effortlessly sliding into the nooks and crannies of your mouth, the Flosser also doubles as a tongue scraper. Worth the counter space it takes up. —R.L.

Using a Waterpik is like visiting the dentist in the comfort of your own bathroom—sans the novocaine. Waterpik’s impressive Promax water flosser is for folks who take their pearly whites seriously. Its five interchangeable tips work for daily flossing, but also for those who want a deeper clean or people who have crowns, bridges, or dentures. It also has a water-pressure intensity range that scales from 1 to 10 and an intuitive setting that automatically knows how hard to go, so you’re not powerwashing your gums in a way that’ll put you back into the periodontist’s chair. —R.L.
